#3077. NBA Cup Preview: Milwaukee Edition

The Milwaukee Bucks are about to kick off the fourth annual NBA Cup with their first group stage game tonight against Toronto. They are the only team in their group, East A, to have not played on October 30; the other four teams—Brooklyn, Toronto, Orlando and Detroit—all played, with Detroit beating Brooklyn and Orlando beating Toronto. Despite being 1-7, the Bucks could actually make some noise in the cup games, and that isn't just hopium-charged optimism, but a reflection of the Bucks' strengths this year (yes, they exist) and how cup games are generally played.

Here is a quick refresher of the NBA Cup rounds, groups, and rules:

The games that are played every Friday, starting October 30 and ending November 27, are NBA Cup games. There are additional Cup games on Tuesday, November 24, and Wednesday, November 25.

Each team plays the four other teams in their group, with two home and two away games.

All Cup games will count towards the regular season standings, except for the championship game.

After the group games have concluded, eight teams will continue to the Knockout Rounds. Those eight teams will be the six group winners plus two "Wild Card" teams, one from each conference that finished with the best record in Group Play games and finished second in their respective group. Convoluted, huh?

The tiebreakers are head-to-head win/loss, point differential, total points, 2025-2026 record, and then a random drawing should all previous tiebreaker fail.

The Knockout Rounds are single-elimination games leading up to the championship, where teams will play for a prize pool and the Cup trophy.

As noted, the Milwaukee Bucks are in Group East A. The standings currently look like this:

As always, we'll cover these games in regular fashion, so stay tuned for more in-depth analysis of each matchup!

With Kawhi Leonard out for Toronto, it appears that the Bucks' toughest test of the Cup will take place later this month when they head to Detroit to take on the Pistons. Beyond these two, Brooklyn is 2-6 to begin the year and Orlando is 2-5, and both of these teams possess a major disadvantage in Cup play: ank in the bottom five in both transition frequency and points scored in transition. This matters because of the significance of point differential: in the Cup, you'll see teams leave their starting five out all game in an effort to score as much as possible, even in the face of a blowout. The Bucks, Raptors and Pistons are all in the top ten in time spent in transition and points per game, but the Bucks are bottom-five in field goal percentage when in transition. This should not shock Bucks fans: pretty much everyone on the Bucks has had a hard time finding their groove and really breaking out. Therefore, should the Bucks start to see some of these shots fall, they may be able to push the pace, wear out Brooklyn and Orlando, and hang in there against Toronto and Detroit.

If those shots do not fall, however, Milwaukee still has a chance if it can play its brand of basketball. A particularly concerning trend for the Bucks in the first eight games of the season is that their bigs are having a hard time finishing at the rim on offense and being able to get consistent stops in the paint on defense. Even with those concerns, the Bucks rank 20th in points in the paint, which is not good, but better than the Nets, Magic and, perhaps most surprisingly, the Pistons, who rank 22nd.

Where the Bucks hold an advantage over all of these teams is rebounding, which both feels right on schedule for a Jenkins-led team and an absolute breath of fresh air for Bucks fans. Currently, the Bucks are third in total rebounds per game and offensive rebounds per game, and fourth in defensive rebounds per game. Only Toronto has the Bucks beat in offensive rebounding, averaging 14.8 per game to Milwaukee's 14.5, but the Bucks hold a fairly sizable lead over their group in every other rebounding category. Plus, even on a poor shooting night, more shots are always better than fewer, meaning fans can expect to see the Bucks push the pace even more and pick up as many second-chance opportunities as possible (where they rank third in the league). So, as long as the Bucks don't beat themselves and stick to the brand of basketball they have been working on in the early season, they actually stand a chance to make a little noise—and maybe even surprise some folks!

You know what? Why not? The Milwaukee Bucks will win their bracket. Yeah, I said it. What's more, they'll go 4-0, baby! After two tough road losses in which the Bucks had chances to take or maintain the lead, the team will return to Fiserv hungry for its second win against a Kawhi-less Raptors team, which lost last week to the Magic, handing Orlando just their second win of the season. After notching the first victory of the Cup against Toronto, the Bucks will travel to Orlando and play the Magic, who will not be able to keep up with the Bucks on the boards or in transition. A similar fate will befall Brooklyn, albeit in more explosive fashion, as the young Bucks will continue to mature and remain hungry and motivated for the cash prize at the end of the journey. Finally, with a chance to advance to the Knockout Rounds on the line, the Bucks will notch a quality road win against Detroit, which (somehow) also struggle on the boards and are in the bottom-10 for second chance points.

Keeping the hopium cranked up, should the Bucks find themselves 4-0 when Group Play is wrapped up, it will likely be due to at least one player breaking out. That will be Brayden Burries, who will finally enjoy his first signature moment. Fans who have been watching all season know that Burries' shot selection has generally been good; he's just had a hard time finding the bottom of the net. The NBA Cup will give him a stage to change this and, coupled with his rebounding ability, he will help find a way to lead his team to victory and emerge as one of the future stars in Milwaukee.

How do you see the Bucks faring in the NBA Cup? Now that it's in its fourth year, how do you feel about the tournament in general?

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#3073. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#3071. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#3068. 6 cooked NBA teams already serving up a lost season

The NBA's 82-game regular season leaves plenty of margin for error for a bad stretch of play. This is a league where 53% of teams make the playoffs, not counting the four other teams who have the opportunity to earn their way into the postseason with one or two wins in the play-in tournament. There are still five months left on the schedule, with back-to-backs and spells of three-games-in-four-nights filling in the calendar.

Most teams can get off to a bad start and still be just fine. Some teams already know their season is over before it ever really starts. Check out our NBA power rankings this week for full coverage of the league.

Just over two weeks into the season, it already feels like there are six teams who are already toast. Some of these teams knew they would be bad coming into the year, while others dreamed of breaking into the playoffs. The unfortunate reality for these teams is they can't even count on a high draft pick next year anymore after the league's anti-tanking lottery reform was put in place this season ahead of the 2027 draft. Of course, there's plenty of time for things to change with only about 10% of the schedule complete, but these six teams already look cooked.

The Kings appeared to have no hope coming into the season, and at this point the only intrigue left is to see if they avoid being the worst team of the 2020s, a stake currently held by the 14-win Detroit Pistons in 2023-24. Rookie guard Darius Acuff is the best thing this franchise has going for them, and he's getting thrown into the deep end from the very start of his career. Acuff leads the NBA in minutes per game and he's one field goal attempt behind 2-time MVP Shai Gilgeous-Alexander for the league-lead in that category. The Kings could probably stand to give some of Acuff's usage (32.5%) to Domantas Sabonis (16.1% usage rate), but they would still be a bad team, and at least now the rookie guard has the ability to learn from his mistakes. Keegan Murray being out again with an ankle injury after only playing 23 games last year is the real bummer for the start of this season. The Kings felt like the worst team in the league entering the season, and they still feel like the worst team in the league. Try to enjoy the Acuff roller coaster, Kings fans, because there's nothing else to latch onto.

The good news is that rookie forward Caleb Wilson looks like he can be the franchise player the Bulls have been missing since their horrible Jimmy Butler trade 10 years ago. The bad news is they might need four new starters around him long-term. It's been a truly disappointing start for Matas Buzelis, who looked like a keeper in his first two seasons under Billy Donovan, but is regressing and seemingly falling out of favor under new head coach Tiago Splitter. Buzelis' inability to beat mismatches when teams put their tiny guards on him is the single most alarming trend of this season for Chicago. Josh Giddey has also been terrible, and likely won't be around the next time the Bulls have a playoff team. No. 15 overall pick Dailyn Swain can't even get on the floor during blowouts. Chicago's perimeter defense is atrocious with Giddey and Norm Powell, it can't grab a defensive rebound (No. 28 in d-rebound rate), and it's dead-last in three-point percentage. This team stinks out loud, but a brutal schedule to open the season will get softer eventually, and Wilson's highlights will at least give the fanbase something to dream on.

It was simply unavoidable that the Bucks would be in for a hard season after trading Giannis Antetokounmpo, and it's playing out as expected. Milwaukee is hanging tough in most of their games under new head coach Taylor Jenkins, but they just don't have the talent to win in the fourth quarter yet. Milwaukee is dead-last at forcing turnovers on defense, and they're near the bottom of the league in both free throw rate and free throw percentage. The Bucks just don't get anything easy right now, which is the true sign of a terrible team. There are some positives thus far: rookie guard Brayden Burries looks pretty good despite a cold start as a shooter, while Jaime Jaquez Jr. and Tyler Herro are both playing well enough to fetch something decent on the trade market. Kel'el Ware, who is playing under 19 minutes per game, has possibly been the biggest disappointment here so far with a lot of sloppy turnovers, undisciplined fouls, and bricked jumpers to start the year. Getting Ware going next to Burries would at least improve the vibes the rest of the way.

The Suns became the feel-good story of last season by grinding out 45 wins with maximum effort on the offensive glass and forcing defensive takeaways. This year's team had to take a step forward after trading their last remaining future first-round pick for Miles Bridges, but it was easy to tell that move was doomed to fail from the start. Bridges hasn't added much of anything to Phoenix at either end of the floor, but that's just the start of their problems. This team continues to play Jalen Green over Collin Gillespie for some reason, even though every piece of evidence points to Gillespie being the exact player they need. The Suns have more of a chance to turn it around this season than most of these other teams provided they finally start playing their best point guard, but their issues in the middle don't have such an easy solution. I loved Khaman Maluach as a draft prospect, but he just doesn't look ready for starting center minutes at all right now. Oso Ighodaro has been better offensively, but he's too small to play full-time center, and the defense completely falls apart when he's on the floor (a ghastly 129 defensive rating in his minutes). Devin Booker is getting paid enough to lift this team through tough times, but he's just not a great enough shot-maker to overcome his lack of rim attempts. Phoenix has some chance to turn it around, but the early returns are highly discouraging.

I was foolish enough to think the Jazz could be a playoff team this year, but my optimism started to fade once Jusuf Nurkic went down with a foot injury in the team's preseason opener. A length injury to Jaren Jackson Jr. followed, which has put an already thin front court in a dangerous spot to start the season. It's a bad sign that Utah's offense has been the main culprit for their struggles, ranking No. 29 in the league right now. There should be a ton of firepower here, but the team has been ice cold from three (31.5 percent, No. 27 in the league), and rookie Darryn Peterson is still figuring out his spots on the floor. Peterson is relying way too heavily on floaters and other non-rim two-pointers right now, and it's tanking his efficiency (46.5% true shooting). There's also just a huge lack of high-level passing on this team right now, and it's one reason why Lauri Markkanen just can't seem to get going. The JJJ trade looks like an abomination. This was supposed to be the season the Jazz finally stopped rebuilding and started getting serious, but the product on the floor remains extremely poor.

The Clippers' future was decimated by the NBA for their dealings with Kawhi Leonard in a cap circumvention scandal, but they still had a chance to be competitive this year. It already feels like that hope is over. Brandon Ingram reported to training camp with a partially torn Achilles, Max Strus is just returning now from a foot injury, and the big man room might be the worst in the league. Meanwhile, Darius Garland is turning the ball over like crazy, and rookie Keaton Wagler might be the worst inside the arc scorer in the NBA (25% on two-pointers). The Clippers turn the ball over more than any team in the league, and only four clubs allow their opponents to score with better efficiency. Worst of all, they owe their 2027 draft pick to the Thunder. I have mad respect for Ty Lue as a head coach, but there's nothing he can do to stop this mess.

#3060. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs. Sacramento Kings

Will the Spurs ever lose again? After starting off the season with a couple of losses, the Silver and Black have won their next six, steadily climbing up the standings.

Even when things don't go well, San Antonio has been able to prevail. In the matchup against the Mavericks on Wednesday, the Spurs shot 36 three-pointers and made just five of them. Only a handful of times in league history has a team shot so poorly at that volume and won. And the poor shooting was only part of the issues. The bench got outscored by 28 points, and only four players cracked double digits in scoring. So how did the Silver and Black keep the streak going? Because they have Victor Wembanyama.

Wemby had arguably the best game of his career, finishing with 52 points, 24 rebounds, and eight blocks. The Spurs outscored Dallas by 23 points when he was on the floor in a one-point win. He was everywhere on defense, deterring or swatting shots. His jumper was off, as it was the case for the entire team, but he made an absolute killing in the paint, where no Maverick could stop him. Wemby willed the team to a win with not much help from others, which is something that only true superstars can do.

Hopefully, it won't take such a Herculean effort from The Alien to secure the win against the bottom-dwelling Kings. Sacramento is having a rough season in which injuries have made a bad roster look more inept than anticipated. The Spurs already made easy work of them in a previous matchup this season, dominating early and cruising to a blowout win. The same outcome seems likely, but San Antonio will need to show the appropriate fear and not assume that their opponent will just roll over or that talent alone will get them another victory.

Spurs injuries: Tobias Harris (calf) — Out, Jayden Quaintance (knee) — Out

Kings injuries: Ben Simmons (Achilles) — Out, Keegan Murray (ankle) — Out, Maxime Raynaud (knee) — Out

The decision to insert Dylan into the starting lineup has been going well enough so far that reverting it wouldn't make sense, at least at this point, but it has had an impact on the second unit. The Spurs' bench ranks 27th in the league in points scored per game and 29th in assists per game. Simply put, there's very little shot creation and firepower outside of the starting unit.

The offensive struggles wouldn't be a problem if San Antonio were bringing defensive specialists off the bench, but that's simply not the case. Keldon Johnson has always been a subpar defender despite always bringing effort. Julian Champagnie struggles with quick perimeter players and is getting exposed often, as he can't keep his man in front of him and has to funnel them to Luke Kornet, having a bad year so far, instead of Victor Wembanyama.

Since the starting unit has so much talent, the Spurs have been able to win games despite lacking quality depth, but a game against a weak opponent on the first night of a back-to-back would be a perfect opportunity for the subs to shine and lighten the load of the starters.

One of the few bright spots of the Kings' season has been the play of their prized rookie, Darius Acuff Jr. The seventh overall pick of the 2026 NBA Draft leads all first-year players in scoring (25.6 a game) and assists (seven a game), and even on a bad team, looks like a true building block for the future — the type of offensive engine that could eventually lead a good attack.

The big issue for the soon-to-be 20-year-old has been efficiency. Acuff has the green light in Sacramento, which is helping him put up the great raw numbers mentioned above, though they can feel a little empty at times. He's shooting just 40 percent from the floor and 31 percent from beyond the arc while turning the ball over often. In Sacramento's last game, he scored 22 points, but it took him 24 shots to get there. In the matchup with the Spurs from earlier in the season, he had 19 points on 20 shots.

Since the Kings' defense is abysmal, the only way for them to beat good teams is to outscore them. Veteran starters Zach LaVine, Domantas Sabonis, and De'Andre Hunter will likely do their job on the offensive end while Malik Monk is expected to do the same off the bench. But Acuff Jr. is the big X-Factor. If he puts up big numbers but isn't efficient, Sacramento won't likely be able to keep up with a superior opponent.

The Spurs will play their first game of the Emirates NBA Cup. They will battle the Kings, Lakers, Warriors, and Trail Blazers in the West's Group C. Sacramento is clearly the worst team of the bunch, so beating them is a must if the Silver and Black, which played in the finals against the Knicks in the previous edition of the competition, are hoping to advance.

If you'd like to, you may follow along with the game on our Twitter profile (@poundingtherock) or visit our Game Thread!

#3056. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#3043. Kristaps Porziņģis steps away from basketball, Warriors amid health issues

Kristaps Porziņģis steps away from basketball, Warriors amid health issues originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Kristaps Porziņģis isn't returning to the Warriors anytime soon, if at all.

The veteran big man announced Friday that he is stepping away from the Warriors and will work with the team to "find a solution that makes sense."

Porziņģis has been away from the Warriors since late September and missed training camp in Hawaii while he deals with a health issue.

Two weeks ago, ESPN's Shams Charania reported that the Warriors are exploring trades involving Porziņģis, and Charania reported Wednesday that Golden State hasn't been paying the former All-Star on his $20 million salary for the 2026-27 season.

Porziņģis has dealt with previous health issues, and his most recent episode began on Sept. 28 when Warriors general manager Mike Dunleavy announced at the team's media day that the 31-year-old would miss the start of the preseason.

Porziņģis issued a statement on Oct. 14, noting that he was improving. But three weeks later, it appears that progress has stalled.

Warriors coach Steve Kerr addressed Porziņģis' absence during an appearance on 95.7 The Game last week.

"We were definitely caught off guard by it in that we were in communication with him over the summer, and it seemed like everything was in a good place right up until training camp," Warriors coach Steve Kerr said on 95.7 The Game's "Willard & Dibs." "And then obviously things went south. Since the start of camp, when he was not here and we had to move forward, I haven't given it a whole lot of thought to be honest, because we're just busy. We have to take every single day and get our team ready for the next game.

"The days are full, the schedule is full, and so I check in with Kristaps occasionally to see how he's doing. But we're moving forward, and that's all we can do. I like our young bigs, Graham [Ike] and Charles [Bassey]. We're excited about playing those guys, and the opportunity that they're going to receive, but everything else is not my jurisdiction. That's Mike's [Dunleavy] and the front office's, and I just come to practice and I look forward to coaching the guys every day. We're competing and seeing what we can put together."

It remains to be seen what solution the Warriors and Porziņģis come up with, but for now, the former No. 4 overall pick is focusing on his health and not worrying about basketball.

#3036. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

#3033. Doc Rivers says coaching Giannis is ‘not easy’

On the most recent episode of The Bill Simmons Podcast, former Bucks coach Doc Rivers shared insight into the challenges he experienced coaching Giannis, saying it's "not easy."

"He plays at this crazy downhill pace," Rivers said. "He has this great passing ability, but he doesn't necessarily have super IQ at the same time. It's really a strange thing.

"The thing he needs to learn is the value of execution… Now he has a coach who's as good as there is at execution."

"He really has one way—downhill. Force, force, force. Every play, he wants to force it. I wish he learned how to execute better. He's one of those guys who has never needed to execute, because they can do it on their own. But that doesn't help the team.

"The thing he needs to learn is the value of execution. How important it is when you're in practice and shootaround to lock in on execution. Now he has a coach who's as good as there is at execution."

Now, I also want to note that Rivers made it clear on the podcast that he loved Giannis as a person and a player, and enjoyed coaching him in general, but that his process was flawed in a few key areas.

On the one hand, Rivers' comments lined up with many of my own critiques of Giannis' game over the years. Namely, he needed to learn to contribute without the ball in his hands and, as Rivers said, understand the value of execution.

On the other hand, it's frustrating that the former Bucks coach had all these pointers for Giannis but apparently couldn't get him to put most of them into practice? Like, you're the coach. That is quite literally your job!

I don't know. Maybe he did try, but by then Giannis' bad habits were so deeply ingrained that it was futile. Maybe there was a top-down directive to placate the two-time MVP at every stop so he wouldn't request a trade. No idea.

Speaking of the org's fear of Giannis asking out, Simmons also criticised the historic waive-and-stretch of Damian Lillard's max contract to free up enough cap space to sign Myles Turner, labelling the move "desperate."

"When I look back on that whole situation, Giannis wanted out, but he didn't want to be the bad guy."

"You know now that you should have traded Giannis that summer," Simmons said. "They (the front office) just weren't ready to do it."

"I don't think Giannis helped with that either, though, in Jon's defence," Rivers replied. "I thought Giannis kept yo-yo-ing.

"When I look back on that whole situation, Giannis wanted out, but he didn't want to be the bad guy.

"He wanted Jon and Wes (Edens) to say, 'We're trading you,' instead of him asking for a trade. To me, that was the game that was being played."

Overall, I didn't think anything Rivers said was false or out of line; Giannis is a superhuman athlete and the most talented basketball player I've ever watched, but he is also a flawed thinker of the game. By the same token, Rivers obviously lacks accountability for the role his own actions played in the Bucks' downfall, but let's be honest, that's par for the course with him.

How did you take Rivers' comments, Bucks fans?

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Implementation Note issued October 28, 2026

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#3030. 'The whole landscape changed': Gaming CEOs see new opportunities post-midterms

Midterm election results have created new openings for U.S. gambling expansion, while raising the prospect of greater political and legal pressure on prediction markets, according to gaming industry executives.

"The whole landscape changed," said Craig Billings, CEO of Wynn Resorts, during the company's earnings call Thursday.

Wynn has been closely watchng developments in the Middle East as it looks to open the United Arab Emirates' first legal casino in 2027. Billings said he suspects the election will have an impact on the "likely trajectory of the conflict in the region."

Closer to home, Billings sees opportunities in Texas. And he's not the only one. On the heels of reporting record third-quarter revenue and raising guidance for its brick-and-mortar business, Penn Entertainment CEO Jay Snowden called out specific opportunities made possible by state leadership changes.

Election Day was "a very good day overall for the industry, and certainly for Penn," Snowden said on Thursday's earnings call.

Texas, in particular, could be a jackpot for the casino industry. In a massive upset, Democrat Vikki Goodwin defeated longtime Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who has been persistent in blocking gambling expansion in the Lone Star State.

"Texas is a fascinating market," Billings said, explaining how many consumers travel to Oklahoma to gamble at tribal casions. "So it's kind of a stealth market that is giant."

Penn has been building a position in Texas for years, Snowden said. It operates two of the state's three Class 1 thoroughbred racetracks and has established relationships with local leaders.

Penn plans to spend more time in Texas and, if lawmakers move toward legalization, Snowden said he expects the company is “positioned as well, if not better than really anybody in the sector.”

Las Vegas Sands also has invested heavily for many years to get casino gambling legalized in Texas.

"When Las Vegas Sands thinks about Texas and the Metroplex, it's really about an asset that will be both for business and future tourism," Sands Chairman and CEO Patrick Dumont told CNBC in an interview in October in Macao.

Dumont recently acquired the controlling stake in the Dallas Mavericks from his mother-in-law Miriam Adelson. He said there is the potential for a new Mavericks arena to incorporate a future casino.

"Whatever Las Vegas Sands can do in the future, that will be its own location, its own site and its own timeline," he said.

Houston Rockets owner Tilman Fertitta, currently serving as U.S. ambassador to Italy, owns Golden Nugget casinos, is Wynn Resorts' largest investor and is buying Caesars to take it private. Following a CNBC interview in Italy, he said, "Of course I want gaming in Texas!"

A new market would draw national sportsbook operators that would likely include DraftKings, Flutter-owned FanDuel, Caesars, Fanatics, and BetMGM, which is co-owned by MGM and Entain, among others.

Though the Texas' state election removed a major political obstacle, it didn't produce a deal. Legalizing casino gambling or sports betting would still require a constitutional amendment approved by two-thirds of both chambers and then voters.

Texas state control is now divided. While Democrats flipped the state House, Republicans kept the Senate. Democrat Gina Hinojosa won the governorship.

Las Vegas Sands spokesman Ron Reese told CNBC it's too early to speculate about what might happen with Texas gaming, but that the company will monitor developments closely.

Truist gaming analyst Barry Jonas wrote in a Wednesday note that he sees mixed implications from the midterm results.

"The Ohio governor-elect Amy Acton's support for regulated online casinos could improve the chances of iGaming legalization," Jonas said. On the other hand, Nebraska voters rejected ballot measures to legalize online betting.

Georgia, where Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governorship, could also provide an opportunity. She campaigned on letting voters decide whether to legalize casino gambling, with revenue expected to be earmarked to help fund education. Sports betting and casinos remain illegal in the state, and earlier sports-betting legislation failed in the House.

Penn could also benefit if lawmakers seek tighter regulations for so-called skill-based gaming machines, which compete with casinos and licensed video gaming terminals, according to Snowden. He said candidates who support skill-based gaming machines lost seats or influence in several states.

Penn operates four casinos in Pennsylvania as well as video gaming terminals in that state and other jurisdictions, and has three casinos in Missouri.

Snowden linked recent strong year-over-year gaming revenue growth to the shutdown of skill games in Pennsylvania in mid-October. Although he cautioned that it was too early to know whether Pennsylvania’s growth would last, he noted Missouri also saw strong revenue growth as its state attorney general pursued the machines there.

In Indiana, voters in three counties rejected a proposed casino license, which Penn sees as a benefit as it limits new competition for existing casinos in Indiana and neighboring Illinois and Ohio. Some gaming insiders speculate that a wave of left-leaning progressives in power could work to limit gambling businesses.

"There's no shortage of noise about where this industry is headed, and the election certainly turns up the volume," said Joe Maloney, president of Sports Betting Alliance. "What hasn't changed with the election is what works, and that's state-regulated sports betting and iGaming, built on accountability, consumer protection, and tax revenue for states. That's our lane, and we'll make that case to every new lawmaker willing to listen."

Democratic victories are likely to increase the pressure on prediction markets, even as industry supporters call out their predictive value in forecasting the election results.

"Every eligible customer should have access to these markets, which are federally regulated through our CFTC-registered Futures Commission Merchant," a Robinhood spokesperson said.

Democrats’ control of Congress gives them subpoena power and committee resources to investigate the president’s family and business dealings. That could put Donald Trump Jr. back in the spotlight as he is a paid strategic adviser to Kalshi and sits on Polymarket’s advisory board.

Congress could also consider legislation limiting the markets’ scope. The bipartisan Prediction Markets Are Gambling Act would bar Commodity Futures Trading Commission-regulated exchanges from offering contracts based on sports or casino-style games. But the bill had only been referred to committee as of the election. A veto-proof law would require two-thirds support in both chambers; no such vote is currently scheduled.

The American Gaming Association has been fighting prediction markets as unlicensed gambling.

"The prediction markets are facing a tsunami of criminal charges, major civil investigations and significant defeats in court," Bill Miller, the trade group's CEO, told CNBC. "In the aftermath of the election, we expect the bipartisan scrutiny of these platforms to further intensify."

"Our platform is an institutional-grade exchange with strong market-integrity and customer-protection programs," Polymarket told CNBC. A spokesman said the company welcomes the opportunity to work with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle.

Repeated requests to Kalshi for comment went unanswered.

Jeff LeRiche, a partner at law firm Husch Blackwell, said he expects more scrutiny.

"Entities with robust compliance and surveillance infrastructure will be well positioned to navigate the increased attention," said LeRiche, who spent more than 20 years in the CFTC's enforcement division.

The state-level legal fight is already underway. Utah has filed criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit rejected Kalshi’s request for a rehearing in its Nevada case, leaving the company exposed to state enforcement there.

New Democratic attorneys general in Ohio, Michigan and Nevada will also decide how to handle inherited disputes.

Ohio’s outgoing AG Andy Wilson helped lead a multistate effort to defend state gambling laws, Michigan’s Dana Nessel sued Kalshi and won a preliminary injunction, and Nevada’s Aaron Ford secured injunctions against prediction-market operators.

New AGs could also decide to follow Utah's lead in filing criminal charges, along with Arizona where criminal charges are pending.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#3025. Yaxel Lendeborg is the NBA’s most entertaining rookie – and he’s not holding back

If a screen-addled pickup basketball player teleported into the body of a high-level athlete, they might resemble Yaxel Lendeborg. The most commonly cited fact about Lendeborg is that he used to spend 12 to 14 hours a day gaming, and didn't debut on his high school basketball team until he was an upperclassman. Professional athletes often have origin stories that resemble the superhero more than the mortal, but Lendeborg really was just like us at one point. Lendeborg has also said that prior to entering the NBA, he didn't really watch it, either. The Puerto Rican forward racked up more than enough accolades in college to qualify for the NBA, including helming Michigan to their first national championship in 37 years, but still. Any of you reading this could smoke the man who may be this season's rookie of the year in trivia about his profession.

Related: Golden State Valkyries celebrate historic WNBA championship with Bay Area parade – in pictures

The 24-year-old Lendeborg is a forward on the Golden State Warriors, a team caught between the ancient, gleaming remnants of the most fearsome dynasty of the 2010s and its future. The balance didn't work out last season, as the Warriors produced flashes of old greatness to win one play-in game before flaming out in the next. Such results are a frustrating waste of Stephen Curry's wonderful form even at 38 years old. Lendeborg is the best hope in a while to help maximize it. But even more importantly, Lendeborg is the character of the season so far. He's spent its first two weeks antagonizing superstars, firing off unselfconscious takes in the press and vacuuming up the spotlight from his blander peers. What a joy to bear witness to an athlete who probably didn't even hear the words "media training" until college.

Lendeborg and the Warriors kicked off their season with a resounding loss to the Los Angeles Lakers, which quickly became the most talked-about blowout of the season so far thanks to Lendeborg making an enemy of the Lakers' star, Luka Dončić. The two jawed at each other throughout the game, in which Dončić torched the Warriors as a whole but somewhat struggled when working one-on-one against Lendeborg. After the game, the rookie took issue with Dončić's style of play – presumably too distracted by playing NBA 2K, he'd previously only watched Dončić via highlights and wasn't familiar with the crafty Slovene's foul-baiting abilities. "You get the foul call. Congratulations, bro," Lendeborg said in a press conference. "Then you talk to me like you did something great. C'mon, dog. That shit is weak. It kind of threw me off because I have so much respect for him. Gone, man. Gone."

Angering Dončić, an all-time great who famously levels up even further when furious, is certainly a choice. (Also, foul-baiting made Lendeborg lose all respect for him? I hope he didn't see this effort from Duncan Robinson; it might propel Lendeborg into manslaughter.) Dončić has been on a historic offensive heater ever since, and if he maintains this temperature and wins his first MVP, he owes thanks to Lendeborg for the motivation. But the friction between the players was fascinating – a player shaking their head at Dončić like a disapproving uncle is certainly a new image – and nobody got hurt. Lendeborg later said that he took his complaints a little far. On the contrary, Yaxel: keep it up, your press conferences are now appointment viewing.

Not even a week later, Lendeborg was drawing fouls against the Nuggets like the best of them, taking 13 free throws and making 12. Just listen to him after that game, openly aspiring to new free-throw shooting heights that everybody seeks but nobody is shameless enough to admit to. I find Lendeborg challenging my staunchly held opinion that not a soul should start a new podcast until the current market desaturates.

It's common for players like this to come out of the gates with color and gradually accept the beige, conflict-avoidant stock quotes spouted by the rest of the league. "Get this man a PR manager," smirks the Redditor, perhaps unaware that they are advocating against their own interests. The more athletes succumb to the monochrome group of the media-trained, the more crucial dissenters become. The longtime reality show Survivor is in decline in part because modern contestants know the game too well, and thus often play the same way. Lendeborg brings to mind a similar truism about professional sports: the less a player cares about the PR contours they're expected to follow, the better TV they make. Luckily, Lendeborg seems to understand his responsibility as an interesting athlete. "I'm not doing that, man," Lendeborg said of media training to the San Francisco Chronicle. "I'm going to stay true to myself, and be honest. I've seen plenty of interviews where people are talking and it's like, 'Man, I don't want to watch this,' you know what I mean? And it's not like I'm click-baiting or click-farming or whatever the heck people are saying."

It's a bonus that Lendeborg is also on an encouraging trajectory on the floor. At 6ft 10in, he provides the Warriors, who rely on exceptional shooting and quick ball movement over height, with some much-needed size. Lendeborg can do a little of everything: defend, shoot threes, pass sharply enough to keep up with Golden State's manic style of play, drive to the basket and lay the ball in through contact.

Lendeborg rained 31 points, 12 rebounds and six assists upon the Denver Nuggets last week, the best performance of anybody in that game. He also helped hold the Nuggets' Aaron Gordon, who often feasts against the Warriors thanks to their small lineup, to seven points. And yet, after that game, Lendeborg had the urge and lack of self-consciousness to ask a panel of former NBA greats for advice on his game during an interview. It's no surprise that some of the feedback was for Lendeborg to keep up the "nasty" edge he'd established. Against the Los Angeles Clippers a few days later, Lendeborg piled up 37 points.

Even in plays Lendeborg isn't a part of, you can see him twitching on the perimeter, calling for the ball and hopping in disappointment when it doesn't arrive. (He may have gotten too excited in a rematch against the Lakers: the Warriors won, but Dončić repeatedly fried Lendeborg, who was benched and eventually fouled out.) In a win against the Miami Heat on Thursday night, Lendeborg drew a foul on Bam Adebayo and hopped up and down with his arms raised in celebration, an NBA finals-grade reaction for a routine play. He recorded a 17-8-6 stat line on the night (with three steals and a block thrown in for good measure) and it already felt quiet by his standards.

Don't raise your expectations for the Warriors just yet – the ligaments holding them together are too frayed for that – but the team have won six games in a row. Behind Curry's gleaming star, much of the credit has to go to Lendeborg. Warriors fans will delight in their maybe-savior, but Lendeborg's personality has given the whole basketball fanbase reason to cheer.

#3022. Family offices boost startup investments in October, backing women's health and caregivers

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

After a slow September, investment firms of ultra-wealthy families stepped up their deal-making last month. In October, family offices made 56 direct investments in companies, up about 30% from the month prior, according to data provided exclusively to CNBC by Fintrx, a private wealth intelligence platform.

Laurene Powell Jobs' Emerson Collective was the most prolific investor of the month, backing three healthcare startups according to Fintrx data. Her investment and philanthropy firm seeks to address health equity and other complex challenges like the energy transition.

Last month, Emerson Collective joined a $555 million Series G for Devoted Health, a healthcare provider and insurer of Medicare Advantage plans, alongside information technology billionaire Azim Premji's namesake family office. Devoted Health, which has counted Emerson Collective as an investor since 2021, announced that it will use the fundraise to expand to five additional states for a total of 34.

Powell Jobs' firm also participated in two fundraises for healthcare data startups BioStack and Collective X Health. BioStack, which raised $9.5 million in seed funding, analyzes patient records across different institutions to create training datasets for artificial intelligence healthcare models. Collective X Health, fresh off a $3.2 million pre-seed round, seeks to close the gender data gap by compiling and licensing peer-reviewed, female-specific data and research.

Emerson Collective is in good company as family offices continue to invest in healthcare innovations from telehealth to drug discovery. In a family office survey released by Citi Wealth in September, healthcare ranked as the second-most-popular sector for direct investing at 32%, only behind AI at 51%.

Melinda French Gates' Pivotal Ventures joined an $82 million Series C for Abby Care, an AI-enabled startup that trains and certifies family members as paid caregivers under Medicaid and Medicare programs.

Erin Harkless Moore, who manages investments at Pivotal Ventures, told CNBC that the firm looks for founders who have first-hand experience with the complexities of healthcare. Abby Care CEO and founder Havi Nguyen started navigating the Medicaid system at age 9 as a caregiver for her sisters and eventually her grandparents.

"We look for teams that understand the problem deeply and have a model built for the realities of the healthcare system as it currently exists. Abby Care is a great example," Moore said. "Their deep understanding of the market allowed them to both spot an unsolved problem and understand how to navigate the regulatory challenges in this space."

For family offices looking to invest in healthcare, Moore said overlooked markets offer a chance to back companies addressing needs that have long gone unmet.

"Women's health is a space that has been historically underfunded and under-researched. We have less data, less funding, and huge gaps to fill," she said. "That can make investing more challenging, but it can also point to where there's real opportunity."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#3019. Montana votes to ban corporate political money. Next stop? Probably the Supreme Court

Imagine a corporation wants to spend $10 million to defeat a political candidate. Under the Supreme Court's landmark 2010 Citizens United ruling, it can spend as much as it wants provided it doesn't coordinate with the candidate's campaign.

On Tuesday, Montana voted to change that.

In a state President Donald Trump won by nearly 20 points in 2024, roughly 65% of Montanans voted to approve Initiative 194 — the "Montana Plan" — embracing a novel legal strategy to curb corporate political spending. If states cannot restrict political speech under Citizens United, can they instead strip corporations of the power to spend money on elections?

The vote follows a record $11.87 billion in political advertising nationwide this election cycle that was split about evenly between the two parties, according to media tracking firm AdImpact.

The voter-approved measure is set to become state law on Jan. 1 in Montana, where voters can make law via ballot initiatives. Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could amend or repeal it, though proponents have pledged that if that happens they will take the fight back to voters in 2028.

Now, Montana is poised for a constitutional showdown that could reach the nation's highest court. If the strategy survives, it could provide a replicable blueprint for restricting corporate political spending across the country. Similar measures are being explored in more than 30 states.

"If a single state can successfully deny corporate power over elections, it proves that Citizens United wasn't the final word on democracy, and we will continue battling," said Tom Moore, a former Federal Election Commission official and current senior fellow at the Center for American Progress who helped develop the Montana Plan.

The Montana Plan hinges on a legal distinction: Corporations may have constitutional rights, but their powers come from the states.

Under Initiative 194, Montana would strip corporations, trade associations, labor unions and nonprofits, including those headquartered elsewhere, of the legal power to spend money influencing elections in the state.

In practice, that should mean no corporate-funded attack ads, no trade associations bankrolling referendum campaigns and no politically active nonprofits spending their own funds to sway Montana voters.

Violators risk losing state-granted corporate privileges such as limited liability, which generally protects shareholders from personal responsibility for corporate debts, until all improperly spent funds are surrendered and legal conditions are met.

"The argument of the people supporting the initiative is, in theory, trying to be pretty simple. Keep your campaign cash out of Montana, or you run the risk of putting your shareholders' personal balance sheets on the line," said Kal Munis, a politics professor at Montana State University who is an expert in the state's politics.

The financial stakes extend well beyond Montana. Known corporate contributions to outside groups in federal elections surged from $13 million in 2010 to more than $600 million by September 2026, according to OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks such spending. That excludes money funneled through nonprofits that conceal their donors.

But there's an irony to the Montana Plan. It had high-dollar backers from corporate America.

State campaign finance data shows Samantha Walton, wife of Walmart heir Lukas Walton, gave $722,300 to groups backing the plan, while LinkedIn co-founder and Democratic megadonor Reid Hoffman contributed $150,000. The campaign's organizing nonprofit, Transparent Election Initiative, is itself a type of dark-money group that generally isn't required to disclose its donors.

And the new law wouldn't exactly take billionaires out of politics. It exempts individuals, political committees, government entities and bona fide news, commentary and editorial content.

Consider Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, the 2026 election's largest known individual donor, with at least $474 million in political giving, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records. Musk could still spend millions of his personal fortune influencing Montana elections. His companies, however, would face the new restrictions.

Moore argued that because corporations derive their legal existence from states, those states should have the power to restrict their political spending.

Opponents call the Montana Plan an unconstitutional end run around Citizens United. If states cannot directly prohibit protected political speech, they argue, they cannot accomplish the same thing by threatening companies' legal privileges.

And critics warn the restrictions would reach beyond corporate giants, silencing local trade groups, labor unions and community organizations on school bonds, tax measures and other local ballot issues.

"Why allow a media corporation to speak out, while denying a non-profit association of citizens the right to organize around clean water or lower taxes?" said Brad Smith, who leads the Institute for Free Speech and is a former FEC chairman. "Billionaires will find loopholes. All this really does is silence Montana's small- and medium-sized businesses."

The first legal test is already underway in Hawaii, where lawmakers enacted a similar law in May. Montana is the first state to embrace the strategy through a statewide vote.

The Hawaii law faces formidable legal opposition. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce has sued to block it, and the Institute for Free Speech is representing the Grassroot Institute of Hawaii in a separate challenge. The national ACLU has also weighed in, arguing in a court brief that the law violates the First Amendment.

Legal and campaign finance experts told CNBC the dispute could eventually reach the Supreme Court, whose 6-3 conservative majority has repeatedly loosened campaign finance restrictions. Just in June, the justices struck down limits on political parties' spending in coordination with candidates.

"Shifting the prohibition into the corporate code is a distinction without a difference," said Michael Kang, a Northwestern Pritzker School of Law professor who studies campaign finance. "The courts will see right through it because the clear intent remains to bypass Citizens United. It seems like a legal sleight of hand."

Montana has lost this fight before. In 1912, voters banned corporate campaign spending after copper barons bribed lawmakers and dominated state politics. The law stood for a century before the Supreme Court struck it down in 2012 under Citizens United.

"The way we look at it, this is states' rights vs. Citizens United," said Moore, of the Center for American Progress. "That's the pitch once and if it makes it to the Supreme Court they're going to need to decide between one of their two favorite legal theories and things to fall back on."

#3018. The Pacers are getting a sobering reminder of how fragile NBA success can be

It is tempting to hand-waive everything that has happened to the Indiana Pacers in the last year and a half as simply the work of some vengeful spirit, unfairly punishing an innocent fanbase who never did anything to deserve this kind of sustained injury catastrophe and lottery luck that would sink any franchise, in Indiana or on the Moon. And hand-waive we shall. But waive enough hands, and the cumulative effect of this bad luck will start slipping through our fingers unnoticed, like grains of sand piling up into a castle the size of the Gainbridge Fieldhouse, threatening to crush us all if we don't make infrastructure improvements.

I was out on the Pacers coming into the year, though I never found the time to publish that take anywhere preseason with the NFL kicking off and the MLB headed for the World Series and then World War III. — you'll just have to trust my editors that I've been talking about it. My concern lay on the fact that a 50-32 Pacers team in 2024-25 had ridden one of the most absurd stretches of clutch shot-making ever to Game 7 of the NBA Finals, lost Tyrese Haliburton to a torn Achilles in one of the most heart-wrenching injuries in NBA history, lost said Game 7, lost Myles Turner to some Bucks stupidity and then went 19-63 (yes, that is nineteen and sixty-three) the following year with a roster I thought … was not that bad.

Every Pacers take I heard last year from people in NBA media more connected than I started and ended with the same two words: "gap year." This 19-63 record was, of course, intentional, as the team tanked for a top draft pick to play when Haliburton was back the following season. But then they made a splashy move, wagering that draft pick on a bold 50-50 with the Clippers for Ivica Zubac, protecting that pick in a specific way that meant if it fell out of the top four, they would lose it. Well … it did, and their 19-63 record netted them nothing in one of the most stacked top fours we've ever seen. Once again: heartbreaking.

Then the actual 2026 season rolled around, and their roster looked solid; at the very least, it would be competitive, if not actually a Finals contender in my mind. And it was going reasonably well for about seven minutes until Haliburton suffered a hamstring injury (like are you serious right now) and the rest of the team got injured along with him. Now 3-6, last week's lineups would make you think they were tanking again.

Their actual players will slowly make it back in time, but the question must officially be asked (no, not "is this team injury prone?" that's a stupid question): do the Pacers have the roster to cope with even one injury, and can this group survive a wide-open but packed Eastern Conference, one with aspiring playoff teams popping out of drawers? Betting markets aren't really sure what to think, with Indiana holding the ninth-best odds in the East at 18-1 on FanDuel. They're squarely out of the top group of Boston, New York and Philadelphia, and not even really in the second group of Toronto, Detroit and Cleveland either.

These are trying realities, and beg one concerning question: even fully healthy, are the Pacers a contender in the first place?

The argument for Indiana coming into the year was basically that Tyrese Haliburton was one of the five most important players in the league, if not actually one of the five best. Sure, there was tanking last year, but many of Indiana's NBA Finals roster played a decent number of games in 2025-26 and they still couldn't get to 20 wins. Unless this was the single most coordinated, organizationally coherent tanking job in history, with unprecedented buy-in from … Pascal Siakam, the only explanation for the 19-63 record was that Haliburton was basically Michael Jordan-level important for Indiana.

And he definitely was the linchpin of everything they did. But none of the advanced stats suggested that the team would literally evaporate without Haliburton. He's not some kind of Net Rating god, and I repeatedly found myself confused as to how the Pacers were losing every game despite a noticeable talent advantage on other teams in their tanking bracket.

That's a long way to say that I don't think that roster was actually that good, and the Finals run was more of an out-of-control party of unrepeatable clutch ridiculousness than it was the actual quality of Indiana basketball. If you accept that (you may not, but just … accept it for now so the grammar of the next clause works), your opinion of this Pacers team in 2026 comes down to how you feel about their upgrades — Kelly Oubre Jr. is a fine depth wing, but Ivica Zubac is the money question. Is he actually better than Myles Turner was in 2024?

With Tyrese Haliburton, I think he can be. Haliburton is a one-of-one elite table-setter for big men, and Zubac needs him on the floor to elevate his offensive game beyond simple, prodding interior hoops. Sidebar: I saw a Pacers fan complaining on Reddit that he doesn't feel like Zubac even looks to shoot the three-pointer even when he's wide open, which I find hysterical since he has made one singular 3-pointer in his 11-year career. But hey, he's open.

So far, though, Zubac has been uninspiring on the Pacers, and he hasn't been a particularly special defender in a few years like Turner was in his prime (yes, that is me declaring Myles Turner's prime over, have you watched a Bucks game recently?). Zubac must be a walking double-double for the Pacers to raise their ceiling.

Without Haliburton, this is all completely moot. They're in survival mode right now, needing to stack wins wherever they can find them until they can bring back a competent roster nightly. But even with Haliburton, I think this roster might just be bringing a squeaky plastic hammer to a knife fight. Like, you might be able to block some slashes and win hand-to-hand combat, and nobody in the eastern conference has a gun … but we have a squeaky plastic hammer. And right now, it has a hole in it.

#3016. Why Todd Monken is 'fired up' to see Shedeur Sanders make first start of 2026

Cleveland Browns coach Todd Monken announced quarterback Shedeur Sanders will start in Week 9 against the New Orleans Saints as normal starter Deshaun Watson continues to work through the NFL's concussion protocol.

Monken, for one, is excited to see what Sanders can do during his first start of the 2026 NFL season.

"I'm fired up to see him play," Monken told reporters during a Friday, Nov. 6, news conference. "I mean, who wouldn't be?"

Coached by his father Deion Sanders at Colorado, Shedeur Sanders made his NFL debut in 2025 and now enters year two as the Cleveland Browns’ quarterback. Sanders' start comes despite Watson returning to practice on Friday, Nov. 6, and logging a limited session.

Monken didn't ruled out Watson serving as the backup behind Sanders for Sunday's game, provided the 31-year-old veteran clears concussion protocol. However, the first-year coach also explained his decision to pivot to Sanders was related to Watson being unable to participate in team drills while working through his injury.

Monken also lauded Sanders for looking "great" in practice and enjoying "a really good three days" in the lead-up to the game against the Saints.

"I liked his confidence. I liked the way he operated," Monken said of Sanders' disposition at practice. "Looked very comfortable in terms of getting our guys in and out of the huddle. Getting to the line of scrimmage was going to be half the battle, obviously going there this week."

"I thought he threw the ball extremely well," the 60-year-old coach added. "So again, I do think there is positives to the amount of reps he got during the fall. Obviously, there was a two-month gap there, but I think there was enough there in terms of our system, how we do things; he's been on every trip. He hears every call that we make. So, if a player is diligent like he's been, then you expect there to be some carryover."

Monken also praised the strides Sanders has continued to make even while serving as the backup to Watson for the first eight games of the season.

"He's only going to continue to get better," Monken said of Sanders. "He really is. He's very diligent, and he likes to play football. He has a natural confidence about him. Just like any young player, they've got to play."

Sanders hasn't gotten to play much in 2026, logging just five snaps and completing all three of his passing attempts for two yards in relief of Watson. That said, he got extended opportunity to prove himself as a rookie, posting a 3-4 record in seven starts while completing 56.6% of his passes for 1,400 yards, seven touchdowns and 10 interceptions.

Sanders will look to improve upon his efficiency in a favorable matchup against a Saints defense that ranks 26th in defensive EPA per play in 2026, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats, and has allowed the fourth-most points per game (27.7).

And Monken is planning to let the 24-year-old quarterback have a full chance to show what he can do on the road in New Orleans.

"I mean, let's go," Monken said. "This is what he's wanted, right? He wants to be a starting quarterback in the NFL. Let's go. Let's let it rip. Let's see what we've got. We're not holding anything back."
