#3014. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#3013. A long-term Elon Musk backer is cashing out of Tesla — and cutting its SpaceX exposure

A key early investor has sold the last of its Tesla shares and sharply reduced its SpaceX holding, taking profits from two of its most successful long-term bets.

Scottish Mortgage Investment Trust, a London-listed closed-end fund managed by Baillie Gifford, said Friday that SpaceX represented 11.1% of its total assets as of Wednesday, down from 15% at Sept. 30.

The position helped drive Scottish Mortgage's 31.5% net asset value return in the six months to the end of September. In its half-year results statement, Scottish Mortgage also said it sold its remaining Tesla stake in April, ending an investment that dated back to 2013.

Scottish Mortgage first invested in SpaceX in December 2018, when Elon Musk's rocket company had a reported valuation of about $31 billion, and committed roughly $200 million through 2021.

The investment surged in value as SpaceX expanded its launch business and Starlink satellite network. By the time of its blockbuster IPO in June, SpaceX represented roughly a quarter of Scottish Mortgage's portfolio by value.

The investment trust has since sold shares as post-IPO lock-up restrictions have lifted in stages, though it said its conviction in SpaceX remains high.

"The decision to reduce the position following its flotation, while retaining a substantial investment, demonstrates the importance of balancing conviction with disciplined portfolio management," Scottish Mortgage said.

SpaceX was a major contributor to Scottish Mortgage's performance during the period, adding 8 percentage points to returns.

The trust's net asset value, or NAV, delivered a total return of 31.5% in the six months through Sept. 30, compared with a 16% return for the FTSE All-World Index. Its share price returned 33.8%.

Proceeds from the SpaceX sales also helped Scottish Mortgage repay borrowings and reduce gearing.

The Tesla sale closes another highly profitable bet for Scottish Mortgage, which invests globally in a mix of public and private high-growth companies, typically with a longer-term investment view.

In a portfolio update in July, the fund said it had invested around $470 million in Tesla, and generated approximately $6.2 billion in realized profit over 13 years.

"Since we first invested in 2013, it has returned 13 times the capital invested, but we felt the share price had come to reflect much of its promise in autonomy and robotics," Scottish Mortgage said on Friday.

The trust also pointed to intensifying competition in Tesla's core automotive business, particularly in China.

Shares in SpaceX and Tesla fell about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday, taking their combined market value more than $420 billion below Tuesday’s close. Investors fear Elon Musk could face new congressional probes after the U.S. midterms, potentially exposing his companies to tighter regulation.

Scottish Mortgage is one of several U.K. investment trusts — comparable to closed-end funds in the U.S. — that were early backers of SpaceX.

Another Baillie Gifford fund, Edinburgh Worldwide Investment Trust, was the focus of a bitter activist campaign by Boaz Weinstein's hedge fund Saba Capital in 2025, with its SpaceX holding becoming a key flashpoint in the long-running battle.

#3006. AI debt concerns grow as ING reportedly seeks buyers for $500 million of data center loans

ING is reportedly seeking buyers for a portfolio of 12 data center loans worth around $500 million, joining a broader push by banks to distribute credit risk tied to the artificial intelligence infrastructure build-out.

The auction is said to include a $25 million piece linked to the Stargate project in New Mexico, which involves OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle, as well as certain other loan assets involving Vantage Data Centers and CyrusOne, Bloomberg reported Friday, citing people familiar with the matter.

ING will hold a bids-wanted-in-competition, or BWIC, auction process, with bids sized at $10 million or more due by Nov. 19, according to Bloomberg. A BWIC is a competitive process for investors to bid on pieces of a loan portfolio.

The sale highlights a growing market for banks to move data center loans to private credit firms, asset managers and insurers as financing for the AI boom expands.

Investment banks typically use a lend-to-distribute model for these types of loans, said Hank Calenti, global markets strategist at SMBC.

"The buyers can range from private credit investors through asset managers to insurers. They would buy these assets for their expected higher returns," he said.

The ING sale comes as fears grow over soaring debt tied to the AI build-out — and potentially disappointing returns among investors.

Those concerns have accelerated following the Democrats' sweeping win in the U.S. midterms, which could see moratoriums or tighter restrictions on data center construction in certain states.

The perceived risk of a construction slowdown sent AI-related stocks into free fall on Thursday, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closing the session down 5.5% — its worst day since April 2025 — and into correction territory.

"Whether banks sell more based on AI data centers moratorium fears should be driven by the cost (discount received) versus the benefit of exiting to free up cash for further lending, where the cost includes explaining the exposure to your board," Calenti told CNBC via email.

"This could be business as usual, or financiers looking to exit before the door shuts."

Pruksa Iamthongthong, head of APAC equities at Aberdeen Investments, said the Democrats' gains have increased concerns around greater scrutiny of data center development, at a time when investors were already questioning the financing and returns behind the scale of AI capital spending.

"We think near-term volatility could continue as the market assesses potential delays to U.S. data center construction and constraints around compute capacity."

Earlier this year, Wall Street lenders including JPMorgan Chase and Morgan Stanley were said to be exploring a variety of approaches to reduce their lending exposures to data center construction.

According to a Financial Times report on May 3, these include offloading portions of debt via private sales to non-bank lenders, as well as through so-called significant risk transfers, which transfer part of a bank's credit risk to investors to free up balance sheet capacity.

#3004. Victor Wembanyama’s historic night set the tone for the season

While the number of precincts reporting was rising on Tuesday night, I realized that watching election returns is similar to watching the NBA Finals. How you feel about the event, and how you will remember it, depends on who you are rooting for.

If you are a Republican and a Spurs fan, Tuesday night could've felt like watching the second half of Game Four of the 2026 Finals, seeing a lead bleed away and feeling powerless to stop it. You probably had trouble sleeping afterwards, as we all did last June, before spending the next day trying to understand what went wrong.

If you are a Democrat and a Spurs fan, Tuesday night could've felt like Game Five of the 2014 Redemption Finals, seeing your side turn around an early deficit, and then run away with things as the night went on. You may have had trouble sleeping that night, but for a different reason than your Republican neighbor or friend.

The important thing is that all of us have been on both sides. Our side has won, and our side has lost. And the same is true for the fans of the other teams. Regardless, they still remain our friends and neighbors, whoever they support. Sports is not politics, and politics isn't sports. The stakes aren't remotely similar, but the people we share our lives with are.

All Spurs fans, regardless of political leanings, certainly enjoyed the night after the election. I played in my normal Wednesday night basketball game, so I had to record the Spurs-Mavericks game, which started at 6:30 p.m. Los Angeles time. Because the game was on ESPN, my recording missed the first several minutes of the Spurs game while the Pistons-76ers game was finishing. When ESPN finally switched from Detroit to San Antonio, Mike Breen was saying that Victor Wembanyama already had 10 points. It turns out that he had his first blocked shot 38 seconds into the game, and scored his tenth point 3 minutes and 48 seconds in. While I missed seeing all of that, this was clearly the start of something special.

And so it continued throughout the first half, leading to this screenshot as the first half ended:

Look at that line: 29 points, 15 boards and 5 blocks. If someone had asked you how Victor did against Dallas, you could have told them, "Great! He had 29 points, 15 boards and 5 blocks!" Then you could've enjoyed following that up with "and that was only the first half".

How about this; Wemby's halftime numbers were better than anyone else's entire game. For the Mavs, Kyrie Irving had 29 points for the game with 7 boards and 5 assists, but shot 9 for 27 from the floor. Naji Marshall had 22 points with 5 boards, 3 assists and 1 block. Neither measured up to Victor's first half. The Spurs' second leading scorer, Dylan Harper had 21 points (8 less than Victor's first half), 5 boards (10 less than Victor had at halftime) and zero assists (although no one on the Spurs other than Victor could make a shot – as we will discuss later).

Of course, Victor played after that remarkable first half, and the Spurs needed him to. When I was coaching, we had an expression when one player was playing well while his teammates were not – "I am sure glad we brought that guy." In the second half, Victor scored 23 points, grabbed 9 rebounds, and blocked 3 more shots. While not as unworldly as his first half, those second half stats were also better than any of his teammates' entire game. And of course, Victor's final line – 52 points, 24 boards (9 offensive) and 8 blocks — was truly historic.

The one thing Victor did not do well Wednesday night was shoot three-pointers – he went 1 for 8. But even that awful number was still better than most of his teammates. Including Victor's 1 for 8, the Spurs shot an incredible (in a very bad way), 5 for 36 from three. Yuck. The Spurs best two shooters, Devin Vassell and Julian Champagnie, went 0 for 6 and 0 for 5, respectively. De'Aaron Fox was 0 for 2 and Keldon Johnson 0 for 3. And while Stephon Castle was a bit better than Victor from three (but still only 2 for 9), he was still 6 for 20 overall. And with all that Victor was doing offensively, he only had one turnover – less than Fox, Castle, Harper, and Johnson.

Not surprisingly, Victor's eight blocks were more than the rest of the Spurs combined. In fact, he had more blocks than the rest of both teams combined. And he had more steals than every other Spur other than Vassell – they both had two. "I am sure glad we brought that guy," indeed.

One other thought that came to me during the game. We all know that Victor's already abundant competitive juices flow even more when he goes up against Chet Holmgren – see, for instance, the 2026 Western Conference Finals. After last night's game, there's at least a possibility that Victor has been reading about how he and the Mavs' young star Cooper Flagg will be competing over the next decade to be the best player in the NBA and the so-called "face of the league". Based on last night's performance, Victor certainly has the lead in that race – and those articles "spurred" him on during Wednesday's game.

I am glad the game was nationally televised for all hoops fans to see. I am especially glad that I remembered to record the game so that I could see it when I got home after Wednesday hoops. I wouldn't skip the weekly game with my crew, but not being able to watch Victor's memorable performance would've been a loss indeed – for me and for basketball fans of all political persuasions.

#2998. 'Glad to be back.' Myles Garrett expected to play for Rams vs. Commanders

For seven games, Myles Garrett could only watch the Rams from a stadium suite, on television or from the sideline as the team favored to win the Super Bowl stumbled to a slow start.

The two-time NFL defensive player of the year was out of action because of a knee injury that required surgery after a season-opening defeat by the San Francisco 49ers in Australia.

But the star edge rusher practiced this week, and barring any setbacks, he will return from injured reserve and play Sunday against the Washington Commanders.

"Glad to be back on the field with my teammates, making an impact, feeling their presence and integrating back with them again," Garrett said Friday.

Read more: 'We can laugh about it.' Aaron Donald really doesn't want to bumble another fumble

The Rams were 2-4 before defeating the Las Vegas Raiders and the Chargers to even their record.

So coach Sean McVay is looking forward to Garrett joining a front that includes three-time NFL defensive player of the year Aaron Donald and other linemen that have performed well as Garrett and Kobie Turner work their way back from injuries.

"It has been exciting to be able to get Myles back into the mix with the other guys that I think have played really well up front and we've got to be able to bring it to life, and it will be fun to see," McVay said.

Garrett, 30, was sidelined for much of training camp because of an unspecified knee issue. He had no tackles in the 27-7 defeat by the 49ers and said afterward that his performance was "the worst version" of himself.

Garret said Friday that was not a "specific moment or time" when he was injured.

The decision to have surgery was made after the loss to the 49ers.

"I was just frustrated after the game," he said. "I knew that wasn't me, and I was being hampered by something. And I knew we had already had that conversation, so afterward we kind of opened it back up.

"I didn't want to miss any time, but I had to be realistic about where I was at physically, and had to make the best choice for myself and the team."

Now that he is back, the Rams can finally field a front that includes Garrett and Donald. The two have combined for 236½ sacks during careers that are expected to land both in the Pro Football Hall of Fame.

After the Rams traded for Garrett in June, Donald ended a two-year retirement two months later to join him.

"He kept us in suspense for quite awhile," Garrett said, "so it was very exciting to hear about that."

Donald, however, did not play in the opener.

"I'm looking forward to the opportunity to get on the field and actually play some real snaps with him," Garrett said.

#2996. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2991. Lions safety Kerby Joseph looking ‘better than anticipated’

Detroit Lions safety Kerby Joseph won't play this week, as he continues to ramp up from a knee injury that cost him over a year of football activities. But as he continues to get more work in and get close to his return to the field, Joseph is starting to turn heads.

Last week, the Lions started his 21-day evaluation clock, getting him worked into walkthrough practices while getting additional reps with teammates after practice. This week, he got his first taste of team action, and he was already doing things Lions safeties coach Jim O'Neill wasn't expecting.

"It's been great. Two weeks ago, it was classroom work, where we were really locked in. Then it was walkthrough and individual. Yesterday was his first team practice, and he looked a lot better than what I anticipated him looking (like)," O'Neil said. "So it was very encouraging. So now he's gotta stack multiple days. He's got to stack them, so today will be a big day for him, but he looked awesome yesterday at practice."

O'Neil credited a lot of people in Joseph's recovery. There's the medical staff that has outlined and executed a recovery plan for the safety. There are players like Dominic Lovett and Jackson Meeks, who have stayed after practice to help get Joseph caught up on the reps he's missed in practice during his ramp-up. But more than anyone, O'Neil credits Joseph's perseverance through the difficult last couple of years.

"He's worked his ass off. He has. He's gone through a mental battle, he's gone through a physical battle, and he's in a great place right now," O'Neil said. "And I'm excited for him, and I know how hard he's working. I know how hard it must be to be at the top of your game and then have it taken away from you. He's grinded to get back to the level that he's used to playing at and at his standard."

The next step for Joseph is simple: stack days. It's not just about how his knee responds to a heavier load, but how Joseph himself can continue to play and grow. As O'Neil is quick to point out, Joseph hasn't really played at a Pro Bowl level in nearly two years, as he was battling through this knee injury even when he was playing in 2025.

But if the Lions can get the 2024 Kerby Joseph back—along with Brian Branch's continued progress now that he's back from his 2025 Achilles injury—then Detroit's back-end could look a lot different and better soon.

"As we get guys back, guys like with Kerby's skillset or (Thomas) Harper's skillset, that just opens up the door to do more stuff with Branch," O'Neil said. "He can go down, he can do dime roles, he can play nickel roles. You can use him more of a chess piece as we get some guys back in the defense and in that secondary."

#2990. Market sell-off hits hard: Advisors say 'stay the course with an eye on buying'

Top financial advisors aren't urging clients to change their portfolios after Thursday's sharp market sell-off, even as retail investors were heavy sellers of individual stocks.

In a Thursday flash poll of advisors from CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council, more than three-quarters of respondents said investors' first steps should be to check their allocation against their goals or stay the course. The online poll, conducted from 2:30 p.m. to 6 p.m. ET on Thursday, was sent to 121 firms and 38 responded.

Retail investors sold individual stocks Thursday as technology stocks tumbled, and the Dow gave back its post-midterm election gains. The S&P 500 suffered its worst one-day drop since April 2025 on Thursday. Analysts linked the sell-off to fears of a data center construction slowdown following major Democratic election wins.

The Nasdaq Composite tumbled 5.5%, closing more than 10% below its most recent high and putting it in what Wall Street regards as a correction. The broad-market S&P 500 lost 4.4%, while the Dow fell more than 1,800 points, or 3.4%.

Yet, financial advisors who answered the CNBC poll overwhelmingly responded that they are not recommending changes to their clients' portfolios. The broader message from interviews with several advisors: review whether your portfolio still matches your goals, but don't let a sudden market move make the decision for you.

For investors who continue contributing to a 401(k), a market decline can mean buying investments at lower prices over time, said Andy Pratt, director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia.

"If you have a 401(k), money is being allocated there biweekly, monthly, you know, whatever your pay period is, and so you're able to dollar-cost average in," Pratt said. "So when the market does sell off, you're entering at a much better spot."

Pratt said a well-designed portfolio may not need changes during a downturn. John Mullen, managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management in Providence, Rhode Island, put it this way: "Stay the course with an eye on buying."

Yet, if investors are panicking, that could be a sign to reassess whether their investments still fit their goals and risk tolerance, financial advisors say.

Carolyn McClanahan, a certified financial planner, physician and founder of Life Planning Partners in Jacksonville, Florida, says she tells clients to follow this strategy, which "works beautifully."

McClanahan said investors should stick to an investment policy that reflects their needs and "tune out the rest of the noise."

Tra Sullivan, a certified financial planner, wealth manager, and chief investment officer at Dallas-based Beaird Harris Wealth Management, said historically the U.S. stock market has often risen in the 12 months after midterm elections, but he views that pattern as context rather than a promise.

He added that when markets fall, "there's always a recovery, and a lot of times, in most cases, more quickly than we anticipate", although past rebounds don't guarantee future recoveries.

Nasdaq futures rebounded in pre-market trading on Friday with a bounce in chip stocks following a sell-off that sent the Nasdaq into correction territory the day before.

Still, if market weakness continues, Sullivan said he brings the discussion back to a client's financial plan.

For long-term investors, Dan Moisand, a CFP and financial advisor at Orlando, Florida-based Moisand Fitzgerald Tamayo, said resilience can matter more than trying to time each market swing.

"Being resilient has proven to be more profitable than trying to be nimble," he said.

Correction: An earlier version of this article misstated John Mullen's certifications. He is managing director, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management.

The NBA's 30 teams are split into six groups of five, with three groups in each conference. Each team plays four group-stage games, two at home and two on the road.

Each NBA Cup game also counts toward the regular-season standings, except for the championship game on Dec. 11. Teams play each of their four group opponents once, with the results determining who advances to the knockout rounds.

Eight teams will advance to the knockout rounds — the winner of each of the six groups, plus one wild-card team from each conference.

#2988. Fake job interview scams like the one that hit Binance are getting more sophisticated. Here are the new signs to watch for

Job search scams can cost applicants hundreds or thousands of dollars. But one fake job interview recently impacted over $6 billion in assets.

Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, announced on Tuesday it recently experienced a security breach impacting $6.34 billion in digital assets. The hack was enabled in part by a job seeker's nightmare: bogus job interviews.

While the Binance hackers had their sights set on a much bigger target than an individual job seeker, job-related scams are on the rise. The FBI's Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) tallied employment-related losses amounting to roughly $363 million in 2025, up sharply from $264 million the year prior, and $70 million in 2023. The actual figure is likely higher, since many victims of such crimes do not report them.

Those orchestrating fake job scams have many potential targets today "because the job search is taking so long for so many right now," says Stefanie Fackrell, a former recruiter at Google and Nvidia. While October's jobs report showed promise as the U.S. economy added 165,000 jobs, far more than expected, many applicants have been struggling through a low-hire, low-fire market for months.

Artificial intelligence can also make scams more widespread and harder to detect.

"Cyber scams in general are becoming much more sophisticated just because they're evolving at the same speed as AI and public access to AI," says Garber. "AI is going to make things seem more and more real."

In 2025, victims reported losses of almost $13 million to AI-involved employment scams, according to the IC3.

Binance's announcement included an abbreviated play-by-play of how its hack went down. Fackrell and Garber broke down some of the warning signs in how the events unfolded, including what job seekers can learn from the incident.

What happened: A software engineer, who was not named, was contacted through "a legitimate job-hunting platform" by someone posing as a project director, Binance said in its announcement. That person arranged for the engineer to have an initial interview with a purported HR representative and a second-round interview with the supposed CEO of a fake company.

Experts' red flags: A headshot that's clearly AI-generated or otherwise looks uncanny, as well as sparse profile information or activity, and few connections should give you pause, Fackrell says. On LinkedIn, for example, you can click the three dots on a person's profile to open an "About this member" pop-up, where you can see when they joined LinkedIn and if they've verified their identity, workplace and school.

Though some small startups may arrange for an interview with a C-suite executive, it's generally unusual, particularly for a second-round interview, unless you'd be reporting to that person, she adds.

What happened: In the first round, the fake HR representative had a live video interview with the software engineer, even sharing with him a social media account with personal photos and videos to build trust, Binance said. The tactic was "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen social engineering approaches," Binance added in its announcement.

Experts' red flags: A recruiter might bring up commonalities with a candidate like the same alma mater or ex-employer to "bring that human element into the conversation," Fackrell says. But sharing personal social media in a first-round interview is strange, she adds.

In video interviews in general, some red flags might include the person moving unusually slowly or not responding to one of your questions, or their visual skipping a beat, Garber says.

What happened: The second-round interview with the purported CEO of the fake company was conducted on "a popular social chat app," Binance said, and the software engineer was instructed to download "an 'assignment' containing a malicious code repository onto his work computer."

Experts' red flags: Interviews commonly happen over Microsoft Teams, Zoom or Google Meet, not a social chat app. "I don't know anybody that's FaceTiming or WhatsApping any of their candidates," Fackrell says.

While take-home assignments, particularly for technical roles, may use third-party systems, don't download software if you're not familiar with it, Fackrell advises. These assignments should be shared via email through a legitimate system, not over the phone. Job seekers should take interviews on a personal device, not a work computer.

Job sites like LinkedIn and Indeed, and entities like the Federal Trade Commission and Better Business Bureau, also have more information about common scams.

You should not have to provide sensitive information or payment in order to get a job, according to the FTC. Legitimate recruiters will reach out from their corporate email addresses, not a personal address. Other red flags include pressure to move quickly through the hiring process or seeing multiple typos in communications.

"Do your research before clicking, responding or engaging with anybody," Fackrell says.

Take a minute to think about how you were contacted and what you're being asked to do to proceed, Garber suggests, and cross-check job postings against the company's official careers page.

"If seems too good to be true, it definitely is," says Garber.

#2986. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2985. Nasdaq drops into correction territory — here’s what typically happens next

The Nasdaq Composite plunged into correction territory on Thursday. History shows more selling can follow in the near term, but a recovery eventually takes hold three months later.

The tech-heavy index slid more than 5% on Thursday, closing more than 11% below its record reached last month. On Wall Street, any decline greater than 10% is a correction. A 20% pullback qualifies as a bear market.

Across 10 previous correction episodes over the last decade, selloffs in 2025, 2022, 2020 and 2018 were followed by further rough selling over the next one month. But the Nasdaq was back to rally mode again after six months for all those occasions except one: 2022.

Overall, the Nasdaq gained an average of 9.41% from the correction bottom over the following three months and 17.03% over six months.

The January 2022 correction was the one that offered a harsher outcome. The Nasdaq lost another 18.3% over the next six months and was down 24.3% a year later.

The catalyst for this correction was Democrats sweeping the House and Senate in Tuesday's midterm elections. The party also made significant gains in Texas, where Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor after campaigning on an immediate moratorium for data center development.

Investors are now weighing whether tighter state-level restrictions could slow data center construction, putting pressure on companies tied to the artificial intelligence trade.

Frank Cappelleri, founder of CappThesis, pointed to past midterm election cycles for guidance and found that points to more volatility in the near term as well.

The chart analyst said the Nasdaq was higher 12 months after the election in all but one of 13 midterm cycles since 1974.

"The caveat is that the path can be a volatile one," he said.

Cappelleri pointed to 2018, when the Nasdaq rallied the day after Democrats won the House. A sharp December sell-off followed, but the index was ultimately about 15% higher a year after the election, he said.

Chip stocks tumbled Thursday, with the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) falling 12% and for its worst day since the Covid-19 breakout of March 2020.

— CNBC's Fred Imbert contributed to the report

#2984. Cavs vs. 76ers: How to watch, odds, and injury report

The Cleveland Cavaliers open up their NBA Cup schedule as they host LeBron James and the Philadelphia 76ers.

This will be James's first game in the arena he called home for over a decade, since he spurned them in free agency last summer. James has received a hero's welcome in each of his returns when he was wearing purple and gold. That reception isn't guaranteed this time around.

The Sixers have shown early on why James chose them over the Cavs. Philadelphia has amassed the most wins so far by putting together the second-best defense in the league — an area that many people thought they would struggle with. The offense has been a work in progress, but they have enough individual shot makers to eke out enough points in the clutch to come away with victories.

The Cavs, conversely, are struggling. They've lost two games in a row and are just 1-4 against teams over .500.

This is also an NBA Cup game. The Cavs haven't advanced out of the group stage in this tournament yet. If they want to do so this season, they'll likely need to win this game, or at the very least, not lose by much.

#2982. Wall Street's fear gauge may be flashing a buy sign

There's nothing like a good selloff to turn a trader into an investor – so the saying goes. It's an adage that echoes the sentiment of an even older one: buy when there's blood in the streets. The maxims ring true after Thursday's selloff in stocks that sent the Cboe Volatility Index (VIX) to its biggest one-day move since April 2025's "Liberation" week for tariffs, according to data from Bespoke Investment Group. In the 19 instances since 1990 in which the VIX rose at least five points from under 20 – it surged more than 11 points from 16.6 to 28.2 Thursday – the median return for the S&P 500 Index three months later was 4.2%, and 4.8% six months later, according to Bespoke analysis that also showed returns were positive 68% and 79% of the time over each period, respectively. "These types of sharp spikes from placid levels are like a line of thunderstorms coming through in summer," said Paul Hickey, co-founder of Bespoke. "The sky quickly darkens and wind gusts, but the loudest thunder and brightest lightning doesn't last long."

To be sure, the data do show the storms can be disruptive near-term. While the median return one month after such events was 2.1%, the average return was negative 0.4%, thanks to a few big downdrafts following volatility surges in 1990 and more recently, Feb. 24, 2020, when the Covid selloff was just getting started. According to X account OddStats, there were only 12 other days in stock market history prior to Thursday when the S&P 500 lost 4% and the Nasdaq 100 Index lost 6% – including one during Liberation week 2025 and three instances during the 2020 pandemic.Tech volatility was even more pronounced as the Nasdaq 100 posted its worst day since March 2020. The Cboe Nasdaq Volatility Index VXN jumped more than 13 points to 38.3, the highest since April 11, 2025. The selling was so intense in chipmakers that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) traded below its net asset value. Yet options traders were still leaning bearish in their trades into the bell Thursday, with 670,000 puts traded out of the total 900,000 options on the ETF. Of the total $1.7 billion options premium exchanged, $1.5 billion of it was in puts, according to SpotGamma.

#2981. Fantasy Basketball Weekend Streamers: Kingston Flemings, Brayden Burries take over as starters

Ahead of Wednesday's game against the Wizards, CJ McCollum suggested to head coach Quin Snyder that Kingston Flemings should take over as the team's starting point guard. He ended up playing just 23 minutes to McCollum's 25, but the move into the starting unit speaks volumes.

Flemings has been able to dish out the dimes all season, but he should be able to provide steals as he plays more minutes, though he hasn't showcased that as much this season compared to his lone season at Houston in college. Flemings has also flashed more as a scorer following a rather quiet start to his career, where he didn't shoot at a high volume.

The Hawks selected Flemings with the No. 8 overall pick, so it would be surprising if he gave the starting job back to McCollum. Flemings has the opportunity to shine this weekend, but he should also be considered as a rest-of-season option.

The Hornets, Bulls, Nuggets, Pistons, Warriors, Rockets, Pacers, Clippers, Grizzlies, Heat, Knicks, Magic, Suns and Raptors only play one game this weekend, so prioritize players from other teams for streaming.

Traoré has started the last five games for the Nets, and been part of the rotation over the last six, and the results have been really good. During this six-game stretch, Traoré has averaged 15.0 points, 2.3 rebounds, 3.7 assists, 1.5 steals, 0.8 blocks and 2.3 triples per game while shooting 53.1 percent from the floor and turning it over just 1.8 times per game. This is another situation where a player is worth streaming this weekend, but he can also be kept on your roster far beyond that.

The rookie took over as the Bucks' starting point guard two games ago, and while the results haven't been great, the opportunity is enough to take a chance on Burries. It will likely come inconsistently, but the opportunity should continue to be there for him as a starter. He was able to post decent steal numbers at Arizona, so hopefully those will start to tick up. Still, he's worth taking a chance on this weekend, and he could end up being worth rostering for the rest of the season.

With all the injuries the Thunder have been dealing with, Stirtz has become a rotation piece for OKC, and the production over the last few games has been strong. Over the last three games, he has averaged 12.0 points and 3.3 triples while shooting 61.9 percent from the floor in 20.3 minutes per game. The upside is that he gets hot and sees his shots go up, but his floor should be some efficient buckets, mostly from beyond the arc. Due to how shorthanded OKC will be for a while, Stirtz could end up seeing his role increase if he continues to contribute at a high level.

He's coming off the bench now, but Williams has been solid for the Lakers this season. He is currently averaging 10.7 points, 1.5 steals and 1.8 triples per game while shooting 53.7 percent from the floor, and his production hasn't taken a hit as a reserve. Williams isn't a high-upside option, but he has been very consistent so far this year and should be a safe streaming option.

Marshall is typically a player that has had success as a streaming option when the starter is injured. That isn't the case in this scenario. He's just been solid and is rostered in just 22 percent of Yahoo! leagues. Marshall did score a season-high 22 points in the Mavs' last game, which is hopefully a performance he can build on this weekend. Marshall should only be considered a must-roster player as a starter if one of the typical starters is injured, but he can still be streamed in from time to time.

Hezonja has been a productive reserve this season for Cleveland, and while he hasn't gotten a start yet, the Cavs have been dealing with enough injuries lately to stream Hezonja in. Peyton Watson (hamstring) is questionable after missing the last two games. Jaylon Tyson (ankle) is set to miss a fourth straight game. Jarrett Allen (knee) is questionable. Tyrese Proctor (ankle), who started in place of Watson on Wednesday, is also questionable. There could potentially be a ton of extra minutes for Hezonja, who has been solid off the bench this season. The Cavs' games are a back-to-back, so it is very possible that Watson and Allen only end up playing one leg.

Mara has seen his role expand due to all the injuries OKC is dealing with, and he even started in their last game. With Jaylin Williams (back) out again, Mara could be in line for another start. Mara has at least one block in every game this season and is averaging 1.9 per game this season. The rest of his production has been inconsistent, but he has shown he can be solid on the glass and will shoot a high percentage. He's a good option if you need classic big man stats.

Matković struggled in the Pelicans' last game, but he's still the starting center, which makes him worth streaming. He should be able to provide a little value across the board, as he has averaged one steal and one block over the past week since joining the starting lineup. However, if you're expecting him to have another 25-point game, you'll likely be disappointed. Matković can be rostered until Yves Missi (knee) returns.

Achiuwa's production has been inconsistent, but he has largely been productive as a starter in place of Keegan Murray, who is still dealing with an ankle injury. Until Murray returns, Achiuwa is a decent option. He is averaging 11.0 points, 6.1 rebounds, 1.6 steals and 1.1 blocks per game this season while starting seven of their nine games.

#2978. What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique

What makes this 76ers team stacked with superstars so unique originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

They're a team of superstars that don't play superstar basketball.

That's the beauty of this 76ers team. They play a brand of unselfish team-first basketball you rarely see from a team loaded with stars.

LeBron James is one of the two or three greatest players in NBA history, a four-time MVP, a four-time NBA champion, a 1st-ballot Hall of Famer.

Joel Embiid is a seven-time all-star, a former MVP, an Olympic gold medalist and a likely Hall of Famer.

Jaylen Brown is a five-time all-star, an NBA champion, a career 20 points-per-game scorer.

Tyrese Maxey is a two-time all-star, one of only two NBA guards averaging 27.0 points per game over the last four years.

Four certified megastars on one team. And somehow they've figured out how to leave their egos at the front door and play smart, tough, disciplined team basketball, the style of ball you just never see on these superstar-laden teams.

On offense, they patiently work the ball around, always looking for the open man or a positive matchup. You've got this bunch of big-name, high-priced All-Stars who are just as happy to pass the ball as shoot it. That never happens.

The 76ers are sixth in the NBA in assists, and they're one of only four teams with twice as many assists as turnovers. Smart, unselfish, patient, disciplined.

When an all-timer like LeBron averages 7.3 assists per game that speaks volumes. LeBron is fifth in the NBA in assists per game among forwards, which is wild. You can see the joy on his face when one of his passes leads to a basket. When one of the greatest players in history has that mentality, it rubs off on everyone around him.

Embiid hasn't really got going yet on offense and still takes too many 3's, but he's also embraced the team-first mentality, and his 4.8 assists per game are more than one full assist above his career average and 4th-most in the NBA among centers or forward-centers. And still one of the most dominating defensive players in the league.

Brown is averaging 27.0 points, which you expect, but he's also averaging 4.3 assists per game, significantly above his career average of 3.8 coming into the season. He's fit in so well so fast you forget its his first season here.

And what can you even say about Maxey? Just a beast. He's averaging nearly 30 points per game but never wastes a possession, always makes the right decision with the ball in his hands and is still finding time to average 4.3 assists per game.

With James, Embiid, Brown and Maxey, the 76ers are the only NBA team with four guys averaging at least 16 points and 4 assists per game. Only the Pacers, Magic and Raptors have three.

But where we're really seeing this group playing out of their minds is on the defensive end, where this bunch of all-stars, including two newcomers, has been astounding.

The 76ers have the 2nd-best defensive efficiency rating in the league, behind only the Pistons, who they play Wednesday night, and they're allowing the 2nd-fewest points in the league, also behind Detroit. Last year, they allowed 123 points per game through eight games. This year they're at 111.

They're holding teams to 43 percent shooting from the field, 3rd-best in the league and their lowest since 2011-12. Their 9.3 steals per game are 6th-most.

All five starters are among the top 25 players in the league in defensive rating – James is fourth, Embiid 11th, Edgecombe 15th, Maxey 22nd and Brown 23rd. How is that even possible?

It's rare to see superstar-caliber guys play as hard on the defensive end as the offensive end. Everybody on this team embraces it. They hustle, they work, they communicate and most importantly they commit to it.

The biggest concern when the 76ers built this dream team was that it would be a team of individuals out for themselves, but the reality has been the opposite.

In a very short period of time, they've learned how to play together. How to play for each other.

And we haven't even mentioned V.J. Edgecomb, who at 21 years old has figured out how to assimilate into this loaded lineup, never force anything, wait for his opportunities and make huge shots when he gets his turn. Edgecombe is at 12.7 points and 3.0 assists per game, and his numbers are understandably down a bit from last year, but if that bothers him it sure doesn't show.

And the role players – Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Jabari Walker – have each adopted the same ego-free style of basketball, supporting the starters however they need to.

Nick Nurse deserves a ton of credit for getting this bunch to play this brand of hoops. It's not easy to get any NBA player to put team goals ahead of individual goals. But he's got Hall of Famers and all-stars doing it and having a blast along the way. They all seem to love playing basketball the right way and they seem to love playing together.

The 76ers are 7-1 and looking like a real threat to win the East, and they're doing it by playing the game the right way.

By committing to each other and not themselves.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2977. Democratic sweep gives markets a reason to rethink path of interest rates

The Democrats' resounding victory in the U.S. midterms has the potential to curb interest rate hikes globally next year, according to market watchers — but the uncertain path of oil prices, U.S. economic growth and fiscal policies will be key.

Central banks around the world are back in monetary tightening mode, with the Federal Reserve, Bank of England, European Central Bank and Bank of Japan all raising rates in recent months.

The shift has in part stemmed from the U.S. and Israel's war with Iran driving up prices in oil and gas markets. The war has also exacerbated a squeeze in global refining capacity which has sent the cost of products like diesel, gasoline and jet fuel soaring.

Traders largely expected big Democratic gains this week after polls pointed in their favor, but there was still uncertainty leading up to the vote about whether the party would capture both chambers of Congress, increasing the ability for lawmakers to challenge President Donald Trump's policymaking.

Crude oil prices tumbled following the clear-cut Democratic victory in both the House and Senate, with international benchmark ICE Brent crude falling 8.7% on Wednesday, closing below $100 a barrel for the first time since September.

That was largely attributed to raised expectations that the result may mean Trump is more inclined to strike a deal to end the war in Iran, helping restore Gulf refinery capacity and improve export conditions. Vice President JD Vance's lengthy statement blaming Republican losses on the Iran war boosted that view.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said there had been "an admission from the Republican camp that the Iran war cost them a clean sweep in the election, which the market has taken to indicate that a swift resolution to the Middle East conflict could help them save face."

"Trump might relish the opportunity to take all the credit if an end to the war leads to a big drop in energy costs."

If the election result "pushes for a resolution of the conflict in the Middle East, that might bring oil down, and that's been an issue for the Fed and inflation, and part of the reason why rates are higher," said Andrew Slimmon, senior portfolio manager and head of the Applied Equity Advisors at Morgan Stanley.

Historic data shows a tight correlation between oil prices and global rates, Ralph Axel, rates strategist at Bank of America, said in a research note published Thursday. Higher oil prices over the next three to six months would likely mean "significantly higher interest rates" in the U.S., but especially so in the U.K. and Germany, he said.

"If oil prices rise, we think rates will too, especially at the front end where long-end term premium is less of a factor and monetary policy expectations play a larger role," he added.

As the uptick in crude prices on Thursday and choppiness on Friday showed, a resolution to the nearly nine-month-long conflict is far from locked in.

"For now, oil has begun to climb back up again, and interest rate expectations have not radically changed from pre-election levels," AJ Bell's Dan Coatsworth noted.

Money markets on Thursday indicated a moderate change in expectations from a week ago, both in the U.S. and Europe, with traders pricing in a slightly higher chance of monetary easing on a one-year horizon.

The effect on a two-year timeframe was more pronounced in the euro area than the U.S., according to CNBC analysis of LSEG data, with rate pricing down 8.7 basis points on the week for the ECB versus 4.6 basis points for the Fed.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage," said Laurel Rapp, director for the U.S. and North America Program at Chatham House. "We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter."

Eurasia Group's Clayton Allen meanwhile told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that while the war with Iran is unpopular at home, leaving the Middle East region without tangible wins would also be unpopular and that he expected an escalation within weeks.

Andrew Bailey, governor of the Bank of England — which hiked rates by 25 basis points on Thursday — told CNBC that energy price pressures stemmed from a combination of obstructions on the Strait of Hormuz, a shortage in refining capacity, and low European gas storage.

"A peace process in the Middle East will not shift all of those elements," he told CNBC's Steve Sedgwick on Thursday.

"Let's wait and see, because we have seen that fall in energy prices over the last 48 hours, but it is a very volatile situation, as we've learnt over the last seven, eight, nine months."

Ken Egan, head of European Sovereign Credit at KBRA, said the interest rate outlook would be primarily impacted by whether economies have enough underlying momentum for existing inflation pressures to become more persistent.

Europe is "heading into winter after months of elevated energy prices, and the full impact is still feeding through to the wider economy," he said by email.

"The concern for central banks is whether these pressures start to push up wages and prices more broadly, or whether weaker demand limits that pass-through."

A key question for the Federal Reserve will be whether the blue sweep "could lead to more fiscal prudence in the U.S. and thus less inflation," Jane Foley, head of FX strategy at Rabobank, said by email.

"It is very early, however, to be sure of the fiscal outlook, not least because many Democrats were voted in to soften cost of living pressures ... so it is very difficult to come to a firm conclusion at this point as to how the outlook for rates will change next year," she said.

The high level of U.S. fiscal stimulus and whether it is about to change is the "million dollar question" right now, James Sullivan, managing director and head of Asia Pacific equity research at J.P. Morgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

"The reasons why you're seeing inflation running high is a combination of a range of factors, from the war in Iran, to tariffs, to significant tightness in particular areas of the energy complex," he said.

"But it's also important to recognize the U.S. government has continued to spend at very high levels, into an economic expansion with low unemployment, and that's driving very specific macroeconomic implications. If that changes, you potentially take the pressure off the rate environment in a way that gives the Fed significantly more forward flexibility."

Daniel Clifton, head of policy research at Baird Strategas, said the impact on the data center buildout in the U.S. was another big factor to watch.

"In 2025, we had major fiscal headwinds, and it really didn't matter because we had such a big capex boom in the AI build-out last year. This year was driven a lot by stimulus," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"If you have an environment where you have tighter fiscal policy — the Fed is raising rates right now — and you have a slowdown in the AI capex build out, you're probably going to reach a policy mistake at some point in mid-2027," he said.

"That's what happened in 2000 during the Y2K tech boom, the Fed was raising rates, we had higher oil prices, and they just went too far, and eventually it had a recession. Our forecast is not for a recession in the United States. Our forecast is that we'll have a mid-cycle slowdown in 2027."

#2975. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2974. Friday Cheese Curds: Packers are plagued by missed opportunities

The Green Bay Packers don't appear to have a problem finding open receivers. Receivers catching the ball, however, is unfortunately a problem.

We've seen the stat six hundred times already, but here it is once more for posterity: Through eight games, Green Bay has recorded 23 dropped passes, eight more than any other NFL team. And don't look now, but that's already more than the Packers had all of last season. As Jordan Love continues to put together an outstanding year, drops are becoming impossible to ignore.

The biggest surprise in all of this is Matthew Golden, who was drafted in part specifically because he is incredibly sure-handed. In his first training camp, months removed from a season where Packers receivers again struggled to make tough catches, Golden reportedly caught everything. In 2026, he has nine drops.

But this isn't exclusively a Golden problem. The issue has spread throughout a receiving group that has otherwise demonstrated plenty of big-play potential.

Ok, let's stop piling on. There's reason for optimism. Christian Watson and Golden have shown the ability to make tough catches generate explosive plays. Tucker Kraft has, too — and I'd expect him to start accumulating impressive plays as he continues to regain confidence following a return from a knee injury. The talent is there, we've seen the proof before, and Love clearly has confidence in his receivers.

That's what makes the problem so frustrating. Green Bay doesn't necessarily need to find another receiver before the November 10 trade deadline. It needs the very talented receivers already on the roster to catch the dang ball.

Until then, they'll continue to be a streaky offense with a quarterback leading the league in passing yards. If the pass catchers can turn it around, though, the passing attack could graduate to the "most dangerous in the league" conversation. That'll also be necessary for this team to survive the NFC North and do damage in the playoffs.

I dunno, can we go back to making them catch bricks or something?

Packers lead NFL in drops by a huge marginThe Green Bay Packers currently hold a wide and possibly insurmountable lead for dropped passes as the midway point of the 2026 season approaches.

Packers must be buyers at trade deadlineThe NFL trade deadline is Tuesday. The Packers haven't been buyers in a long time but there is a green-and-golden opportunity to make a move in the wide-open NFC.

#2973. AI crush is powering one stock to sky-high valuations

The 2026 midterm elections have profoundly altered the national regulatory environment, most notably with Democrats securing control of both chambers of the U.S. Congress and notching big victories in traditionally business-friendly states like Texas and South Carolina.

Over the past two decades, Southern states like Florida and Texas have attracted massive corporate migration from blue states. With less regulation and lower taxes. Those same benefits also attracted many people seeking lower taxes, lower housing costs, and better economic opportunities. With this political shift, relocated companies may now face a landscape increasingly focused on energy and building constraints, and tighter legislative oversight.

Information technology, semiconductors, and traditional energy sectors experienced immediate headwinds following the election. Companies at the forefront of the AI boom, such as CoreWeave, Tesla, and SpaceX, saw share prices slide as markets priced in tighter Democratic regulatory oversight and potential restrictions on AI deployment. Senator Bernie Sanders, for example, reposted his efforts to advance a data center moratorium proposal he co-authored with New York Congresswoman and socialist Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez.

The market is suddenly realizing that, between shifts in Washington and in states like Texas, the fastest-growing area of the U.S. economy might grind to a halt. Over half of the U.S. hyperscale data center pipeline could now face heightened regulatory friction. To build new facilities, even assuming they get permitted at all, developers may face tax increases, strict "bring your own power" mandates, or new air-permit delays for on-site gas plants. As all-in construction costs rise and timelines extend, immediate demand for advanced chips snowballs down the hardware supply chain.

Traditional oil and gas majors sank, while solar and green energy stocks surged, driven by expectations that a Democratic Congress will resume pushing subsidies for them. Also, the ongoing U.S.-Israel war with Iran has driven crude prices sharply higher throughout the year. With Democrats taking control of Congress, President Trump faces a legislative body that can severely constrain his military options and push back on defense budgets. Tehran almost certainly recognized that a politically weakened Trump administration would have a greater incentive to seek a negotiated deal, something Secretary of State Marco Rubio has now indicated is preferable to any escalating kinetic options. Consequently, crude prices plunged, with Brent dropping below $95 per barrel.

For similar reasons, defense contractors took market hits due to anticipated congressional resistance to the $1.5 trillion Pentagon budget. Although healthcare - as measured by XLV at least came in today, it remains above pre-election levels, along with grocery stocks such as Walmart, Costco, Kroger, and Dollar General, on expectations that a Democratic Congress might boost federal spending in those areas.

This may help explain why Costco was one of the relatively few stocks to close higher on Thursday, and why it also ranked as the 15th most active single-stock option (in notional terms), ahead of names like JP Morgan, Marvell, Oracle, Eli Lilly, and Dell.

At roughly 46x forward earnings, Costco (a perennial on the "Holly Index (MELLOY, CAN WE LINK TO THIS") isn't cheap, but then it is never cheap. The average forward multiple is actually a few turns higher at over 49x. So what were folks trading? Mostly short-dated call options. One possible reason? Costco isn't just trading at a relatively high multiple; as usual, it is also simply a high-priced stock in dollar terms, at over $1,065 as of Thursday's close. A "round lot" (100 shares) would therefore cost over $100,000 to initiate. Like many things packaged at Costco, that might be a little more than some buyers need (or want) to spend.

Alternatively, call options let investors gain exposure to 100 shares for far less than buying the shares. Buying the January 1060 calls, for example, would cost about $53.45, or $5,345 for upside participation on 100 shares of stock above the $1,060 strike price. That may seem more palatable, but a keen observer might notice that prior highs occasionally present some technical resistance, and Costco topped out at about $1,090 back in mid-May. By selling a call at or near that level, such as the January 1090s at 39.65, one could reduce the cost of the trade even further, expecting a pause before new highs. This would reduce the cost of the trade to $13.80, or just under 1.3% of the current stock price.

I won't pretend $1,380 is a small outlay for a one-lot call spread on a much-loved stock trading at 46x forward earnings. Nor is $520 for 8 6-ounce A5 Wagyu filet mignons (a Costco online only deal available now). But relatively, both the steaks at Costco and the options on Costco are very fairly priced. But it does provide participation in 100 shares of Costco between the strikes, for only 30% more than the cost of a single share of the underlying stock.

#2972. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney
