#2939. Steph Curry earns ‘healthy monetary value’ from Klay Thompson

Steph Curry and Klay Thompson squared off Thursday night for the eighth time in opposing uniforms. But it was a matchup earlier in the week that really got their competitive juices flowing.

A few days before Curry led the Warriors to a comeback win over Thompson's Heat, he notched another victory via the former teammates' collegiate proxies. In a battle of Splash Brother alma maters, Davidson opened its season Monday night with a win over Washington State.

"There was a healthy monetary value to that," Curry quipped after Golden State's 120-100 win.

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry, left, talks with Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson after an NBA game Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026, in San Francisco. AP Photo/Tony Avelar

Thompson confirmed that he most definitely heard from Curry after his Cougars took a 76-61 home loss to the Wildcats. In fact, that was all he heard leading into their own showdown.

"The only conversations we've had are Davidson beating the Washington State Cougars and me getting the best end of that bet," Curry said. "It actually snuck up on me. I knew the schedule, but the day before, I texted him, just, 'It's a great day in basketball history that the Wildcats are headed up to the Palouse to play his team.' … Glad I came out on the right side."

A few days later, Curry came out on top again.

Golden State fell behind by double digits in the first half for the fourth consecutive game but pulled out its sixth win in a row. Curry finished one shy of his fourth straight 30-point game.

"What Steph's doing," Thompson said, "I might not have appreciated it as much as his teammate because I got to see it every night so it became normal, but to see it as an opponent, it's incredible, especially that he's nearing 40 and moves like a young athlete."

Stephen Curry (30) of the Golden State Warriors handles the ball against Klay Thompson (11) of the Miami Heat on Nov. 5, 2026, at Chase Center in San Francisco. NBAE via Getty Images

Thompson, averaging 24.7 over his previous three games, was contained to 11 points on 3-of-10 shooting. He had been 19-for-36 from 3 over his past three games but drained only two of his seven attempts from distance, including an air ball on his contested first heave.

In a twist on their previous matchups, the old Splash Brothers ended up defending each other for a good portion of the game. Thompson drained one of his 3s on Curry and picked up another three points at the foul line after getting Curry to bite on a pump fake.

Only two of Curry's 19 attempts came against Thompson. He beat him on one drive to the basket with an up-and-under, and Thompson got the upper hand on the other with a block.

Klay Thompson #11 of the Miami Heat shoots against Stephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors in San Francisco on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. Getty Images

"It's still surreal playing against him," Curry said. "It's always gonna be that way cause we've gone through so many battles together. And it's kind of cool even at this stage to guard him.

"You've been on the other side of it for so long, so there's an appreciation there. I think mostly it's just an excitement of the fact that we're still able to have battles. You know that every one that you get is one closer to the end. So you try to appreciate it even though there's definitely a competitive spirit out there."

Stephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors and Klay Thompson #11 of the Miami Heat look on during a game, Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026, in San Francisco. NBAE via Getty Images

Still, the loss could only sour the night so much.

"It still feels special every time," Thompson said after his fifth trip back, "especially receiving the love from the fanbase and seeing people that I spent years with. It'll always feel like home. …

"It was fun, really fun. Steph's super crafty, still to this day. It felt good to block his shot. He made a great up and under on me. He had an incredible night."

Thompson lamented Miami's tough travel schedule, playing at Golden State a night after knocking off the Clippers in double overtime. Thompson's corner 3 at the buzzer sealed the win.

Stephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors and Klay Thompson #11 of the Miami Heat look on during a game at Chase Center in San Francisco on Nov. 5, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Warriors coach Steve Kerr said that he had been watching live.

"I texted Draymond [Green] right after," Curry said. "Just told him Killa Klay. It's always great to see him having fun but then you obviously wanted to come in and shut his water off tonight."

#2938. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2937. OpenAI, Arm and Qualcomm executives react to post-midterm AI sell-off

As a post-midterm rally Wednesday turned into a steep sell-off Thursday, tech executives sought to look through the notion that potential data center constraints could be catastrophic for their businesses.

In response to a Democratic sweep of Congress as well as a sizeable number of state governorships and legislatures in the midterm election Tuesday night, stocks initially rallied Wednesday, posting their best day in more than 18 months as President Trump accepted the blowout results with little fanfare.

The tide quickly turned Thursday, however, with stocks selling off sharply as investors feared a potential slowdown in the data center build-out after wins by candidates in favor of data center regulation in states like Texas and the possibility that the artificial intelligence trade may be past its peak. Tech led the sell-off, with the "Magnificent Seven" losing over $1 trillion in aggregate market cap.

Stocks recovered some of their losses Friday.

Here's how tech executives reacted to the midterms and sell-off threat:

Haas characterized the postelection fear as "very, very short term thinking" and not likely to be a major threat to Arm's business in the long term, but said the U.S. must keep investing in the data center build-out before the rest of the world catches up.

"If the U.S. hesitates in any way, shape, or form in terms of data center build-out, the rest of the world is ready and willing. There is just huge demand for this capacity," Haas told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Thursday.

Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen Friday that OpenAI will continue investing in data center infrastructure after voters resoundly rejected data centers in their backyards during the midterm election. The company will also focus on community concerns, including electricity. "We will never raise your electricity bill," Friar said."When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand. It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital," Friar said.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them."

Amon said the anxiety about the future of the data center build-out doesn't pose a significant threat to the chipmaker's business, as data center infrastructure is just one segment. Qualcomm slid 9.49% in Thursday's sell-off before recovering some losses Friday.

"The whole market is down, but I think the Qualcomm story is a little bit different," Amon told CNBC's Jon Fortt Thursday. Unlike traditional PC and data center chipmakers, Qualcomm has historically focused on low-power consumption computing. "The story of Qualcomm is what we have done outside that on the data center specific question."

Levie told "Squawk on the Street" Thursday that productivity gains meant AI "is clearly going in one direction," but said a tendency for industry messaging to coalesce around "doomerism" can exacerbate the public perception problems AI companies face.

"I think it's a little bit easier to rotate it toward maybe the more risk-oriented messages, and that's something that our industry needs to work on clearly. But ultimately, obviously, the consumer needs to see the direct benefit, and that's something that will just take time," said Box's Levie.

Risher said the dour market sentiment wasn't concerning for Lyft's business, as consumer sentiment and bookings remained strong. "I wouldn't get too distracted about the short term stuff of what's going on in the market. If I look at the consumer, for example, we just had the biggest Halloween we've ever had," Risher told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Friday.

#2936. Yahoo Perfect 5: Luka Dončić, Evan Mobley and Walker Kessler lead my Friday lineup

Yahoo Sports is giving fans another way to play fantasy basketball with Perfect 5, a free-to-play game featuring each week's NBA on Prime doubleheader. The rules are simple: pick five players, stay within a $15 budget and rack up fantasy points through scoring, rebounds, assists, defensive stats and 3s.

Friday's slate features the 76ers at Cavaliers (7:30 p.m. ET) and Trail Blazers at Lakers (10 p.m. ET). There's plenty of star power, but the key to building a competitive lineup is finding the right matchups and the values in the $1 tier.

Using RotoWire's data and Yahoo projections, I ran 20,000 simulations of the slate. I'm paying up for Luka Dončić and leaning on two $4 bigs with great setups. Here's my lineup:

In my sims, this group averaged 160.08 fantasy points and finished in the top 1% of the field 21.7% of the time, the best rate of any lineup I tested.

Luka Dončić – PG, Los Angeles Lakers – ($5, 76% picked)

The chalkiest pick in the pool feels necessary because he's been one of the best, if not the best, players to start the season. Dončić is averaging 38.5 points, 8.2 rebounds, 8.0 assists and 5.9 3s, and he's gone for 40+ points in three straight games. Portland is middle of the pack against point guards, but when a guy is as hot as Luka, with a usage rate north of 40%, you play 'em.

He was in the single best lineup in 55% of my simulations, more than any other player. Portland beat the Lakers 136-129 in L.A. on Oct. 27, even though Luka went for 41 points, 9 assists and 6 3s. He'll want this one back. Lock him in.

Evan Mobley – PF, Cleveland Cavaliers – ($4, 12% picked)

Mobley is projected for 42.46 fantasy points at $4, within about a point of Donovan Mitchell, whose salary is $5. He's putting up 18.8 points, 8.2 rebounds, 3.5 assists and 1.3 blocks, while hitting 2.1 3s a night. The matchup is neutral (Philly ranks 19th in fantasy points allowed to power forwards), and his usage has ticked up to 23.6% over the last three games.

Jarrett Allen is questionable with a right knee contusion, after leaving Monday's contest after just seven minutes. If Allen sits, Mobley soaks up even more of the paint work against a light Philly frontcourt. At 12% picked, he's still the best combination of value and leverage on the slate.

Walker Kessler – C, Los Angeles Lakers – ($4, 12% picked)

Portland gives up 60.4 fantasy points per game to centers, the second-most in the league and about eight more than average. He's putting up 14.4 points, 10.2 rebounds and 2.7 blocks per night. I originally had Deni Avdija in this spot, but the matchups flipped it. The Lakers are one of the toughest defenses against small forwards, while Kessler gets one of the league's softest frontcourts. Now Kessler grades out better, and he's picked at the same 12% rate. Avdija is still a fine pivot if you'd rather have his playmaking, and that version of this lineup was a close second.

Sidy Cissoko – SF, Portland Trail Blazers – ($1, 41% picked)

Cissoko is the most popular non-Luka pick on the board. He's projected for 17.58 fantasy points at a $1 salary; I think he might be the best points-per-dollar mark on the slate. While the matchup isn't great (the Lakers are 26th in fantasy points allowed to small forwards), his minutes have held to around 21 a night. Trusting the math here, as he showed up in the optimal lineup in 45% of my sims.

Meleek Thomas – SG, Cleveland Cavaliers – ($1, 14% picked)

Here's where I'm going against Yahoo projections. Thomas is projected for only 12.00 fantasy points: that's too low. Over the last three contests, he's averaging 13.0 points, 2.3 rebounds, 1.0 assist and 2.6 3s, which works out to roughly 21 fantasy points. His minutes match his production: he played 22.3 minutes over that span with a 25.4% usage rate.

He was listed with right foot soreness on a recent injury report, so give it a look before tip. I'd originally pegged Kentavious Caldwell-Pope as the safe swap, but his minutes have dropped to 21, 13 and 18 in his last three, so I'm sticking with Thomas.

Not quite. Using Yahoo's projections, the highest-scoring possible lineup is Dončić, Mobley, Jaylen Brown, Cissoko and Caldwell-Pope at 163.64 fantasy points. Mine comes in 3.56 points behind it.

The max lineup is a good build. It averaged nearly 164.0 points in my sims and finished in the top 1% about 13% of the time. Brown is a strong play, since Cleveland gives up the fifth-most fantasy points to small forwards. The weak spot is Caldwell-Pope and his shrinking minutes. The other problem is that everyone reading the same projections gets to this exact five, and it's the chalkiest high-projection build. In a game where you're trying to finish top-three against the whole Yahoo community, tying a bunch of people doesn't help much. Swapping Brown for Kessler and Caldwell-Pope for Thomas gives up 3.56 projected points but buys better matchups and more separation.

You can start building your Perfect 5 roster for Friday's slate now, including going up against me in my Perfect 5 group. Good luck!

#2935. ‘He doesn’t necessarily have a super-high IQ’: Doc Rivers slams Giannis Antetokounmpo

Former Milwaukee Bucks coach Doc Rivers officially stepped down as head coach in April after a long NBA tenure. Shortly after, longtime Bucks forward Giannis Antetokounmpo was traded to the Miami Heat to embark on the back half of his career.

Already nine games into the regular season, the Heat hold a 3-6 record as Antetokounmpo averages 27.3 points per game, 12.7 rebounds, and 5.6 assists to lead the team in both points and rebounds, a role he is very familiar with. But even as part of a new team and new system, it looks like we're seeing a repeat of the days in Milwaukee, with the G-man putting on a show and the team struggling to execute.

Recently, Rivers sat with Bill Simmons on The Bill Simmons Podcast to address some of his grievances with Antetokounmpo and the highs and lows of coaching during their time in Milwaukee.

"He's not easy. Because he plays at this crazy downhill pace. He's really a difficult guy," Rivers said in a clip shared by Ahmed/The Ears on X. "He has this great passing ability, but he doesn't necessarily have a super-high IQ at the same time. It's really a strange thing, and he really has only one way: downhill, force, force. Every play, he wants to force it."

"I wish he'd learned how to execute better. He's one of those guys you get a lot of them from the US, AAU guys that have never needed to execute because they can do it on their own. But that doesn't help the team," Rivers continued.

"The thing he needs to still learn is the value of execution, how important it is, when you're in practice and at shootaround, to lock in on execution. Now he has a coach who's as good as there is in execution," he added.

Simmons interjected, giving the Greek Freak the benefit of the doubt for being one of the most athletically talented players remaining in the NBA; sometimes being aggressive works.

"Yeah, but that's good for him, and what a lot of players don't understand is, 'Yeah, I don't need that, I can do that.' Yeah, you can, but for the team to work, you have to learn how to execute," Rivers added.

Rivers was officially inducted into the Naismith Memorial Basketball Hall of Fame in August, and since stepping down from coaching has taken up a role with NBC Sports as an NBA game analyst for the 2026-27 season. With the season just beginning, Antetokounmpo has plenty of time to get adjusted to Heat head coach Erik Spoelstra's methods, as he is not shy about execution or winning.

#2934. OpenAI will keep investing in AI data centers despite election results, CFO tells CNBC

OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar on Friday said the company will continue to invest in artificial intelligence infrastructure, even after voters strongly opposed data centers during Tuesday's midterm elections.

"When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand," Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen in an interview at the Citi TMT Leadership Summit in Pebble Beach, California. "It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital."

Out of 41 local ballot measures across 10 states, voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures in all but one, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday. Opposition to the facilities mounted swiftly over the summer as Americans grew worried about their potential to harm the environment, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss.

Investors have turned skeptical on the AI trade of late due to a combination of higher borrowing costs, concerns about soaring debt and public backlash toward the industry. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, led by a plunge in shares of the chipmakers and neocloud companies most closely tied to the AI buildout. The stocks rebounded some on Friday.

OpenAI depends on data centers to train its AI models and serve customers, and Friar said she doesn't think the election results dramatically changed anything for the company. On Thursday, OpenAI published a policy blueprint and a series of commitments that it pledged to apply to company-owned and -operated data centers in the future.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them," Friar said.

OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, and Friar said Friday that the company would continue to look into a 2027 debut. Anthropic, OpenAI's chief rival, is trying to go public first, with an aim to hit the Nasdaq in mid-November.

Anthropic's revenue in the first nine months of the year jumped roughly 1,200% to $33.2 billion, according to its prospectus. The company said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

OpenAI is in talks to raise funding at a $1.25 billion pre-money valuation, Friar said Friday. She said "a good CFO always raises money when she can" and that the round is ongoing.

The company has closed an initial $11 billion portion of that funding round, CNBC confirmed on Friday, and it could raise up to an additional $9 billion in the coming weeks. Bloomberg was first to report the $11 billion portion.

"The demand for AI is real," Friar said. "I don't think anyone's arguing with that, or if they are, I think it's a losing argument."

#2933. Patience, professionalism paying off for NY Giants’ RB Devin Singletary

New York Giants running back Devin Singletary was inactive from Weeks 3-5. In Weeks 1,2, and 6 — while the Giants were trying to figure out a workable backfield rotation — Singletary carried the ball only 13 times.

Yet, there he was on the final play of overtime in Week 7 against the Houston Texans, the last man standing from what had been a four-man competition. An unhappy Najee Harris had been cut days before. Tyrone Tracy suffered a hamstring injury in the first half that would land him on IR. One play earlier, Cam Skattebo had injured his own hamstring on a career-best 42-yard run.

So, it was Singletary left to take the handoff from J.J. McCarthy at the Texans' 30-yard line in a tie game. Suddenly, he was in the secondary. Then, after a couple of nifty cuts, he was in the end zone and the Giants had won a game.

In the post-game madness, a jubilant Singletary and head coach John Harbaugh landed in a heap on the ground.

Singletary said this week that "I still don't know" if Harbaugh tackled him, or vice versa.

"I think we just tripped," Singletary said. "As Coach would say, it's football joy. You go through a lot to be able to get to football joy. We were just so happy, so excited. He was telling me how much he loved me. I was telling him how much I loved him."

Singletary said there "was never no bad feelings" between himself and Harbaugh because of the eight-veteran's lack of opportunities. Singletary, 29, understand that it was "business."

Of course Singletary, who has more than 1,100 carries and 5,000 NFL rushing yards, didn't like not playing. He did appreciate Harbaugh's honesty.

"One thing I do respect about him, he kept it real with me. You don't always see that in the league. That's why I always got so much respect for him for that reason," Singletary said. "It was just, this is what it is right now. I need you to stay patient and we'll see how it plays out. Simple as that.

"I was like, I appreciate that. Some people won't even tell you that. They'll just leave you there with no answers, with nothing."

It played out such that Singletary got to play the hero against the team he gained a career-high in rushing yards for in 2023.

"I wouldn't say frustrating," Singletary said in reference to his lack of playing time. "It's like, yes, part of it is how the game goes. It's a lot of competition. It's a deep backfield. But as far as being a pro, that's me. That's how I was brought up.

"I'm going to always go about my business the right way. Stay ready, because when that opportunity comes, you want to be ready to seize the moment instead of being so mad or whatever it is, sad or whatever, and you can't capture the moment when it's here."

After the Texans game, Harbaugh said Singletary's lack of playing time was "on me."

"We were looking for the formula, and Devin wasn't — he was the odd man out for a number of weeks. Not because of anything he did or didn't do, he just was the odd man out, and that's exactly what I told him," Harbaugh said. "He asked me, 'what can I do?' I said, 'there's nothing you can do, you're doing everything you can do, and your time will come'. It was going to come. And look where it came, right there, and he came through."

Offensive coordinator Matt spoke this week about how appreciative he is of Singletary's professionalism.

"We went through the whole thing with the different running backs and four or five guys and he's been here from day one, from OTA's, and through training camp, through the season, wasn't playing, wasn't active. And he'd sit back in practice when he wasn't playing and not say a word, but just be completely professional," Nagy said. "And we talked, and we talked about great things happen to great people and I just think back to that Texans game, and when he scored that touchdown at the end, the discussions that we had in practice when he wasn't playing, he never became negative, he never made it about him. He was as great of a professional player as there was, and look what happened.

"And I think it's a story for young kids, it's a story for vets to be patient, to understand things happen for a reason and when he scored that touchdown, I couldn't think but anything of how he handled himself the entire last five weeks. And we talked about it in the locker room, and I think for him, what a credit and we're lucky to have him."

Baseball fans might recall the name Tommy Henrich, an outfielder/first baseman for the New York Yankees from 1937-1950. His nickname was 'Old Reliable'. In a way, it is what Singletary has become for Harbaugh's Giants.

Now, with Tracy on IR and Skattebo perhaps unable to play Sunday against the Eagles, Singletary is likely to be the lead back.

"I know what I can do. I want to be able to display that," Singletary said. "We'll see what this week brings, but yeah, I'm excited."

#2932. The Knicks have a bench guard conundrum

When you're the defending champions, there are not many holistic things to complain about.

Sure, the Knicks started 0-3 with a very unsettling offense, but all they needed was their medicine (the Nets and Bulls) to get back to .500?

Worried about losing to Philadelphia twice in October? Joel Embiid's plantar fasciitis has already acted up; calm down.

Any "problems" this early in the season are either short-term, regarding the looming extension talks, or good problems, which are more conundrums than actual problems.

The biggest of those conundrums has to do with the Knicks' rotation, specifically with their copious amounts of guard depth.

Jalen Brunson is the head of the snake, but after him? There are a lot of players with minutes who could fluctuate on any given night.

Tyler Kolek and Jose Alvarado duel over backup ball-handler minutes. Jordan Clarkson and Deuce McBride grapple over bench two-guard minutes. Landry Shamet's strong play is actively pushing starters Mikal Bridges and Josh Hart over their minutes with the three entrenched parts of the team's nucleus.

It does qualify as a good problem to have, as injuries will undoubtedly give each of these guys ample opportunity over the course of 82 games, but when they're all healthy, like they are right now? It probably gives Mike Brown a bigger headache than watching Andre Drummond attempt layups.

All of these guys provide something that the Knicks could use more of, but have warts that can limit their playing time.

Kolek is the best true playmaker, but he's a poor defender, and his jump shot has been consistent throughout his young NBA career. This basically also describes Alvarado, but the defense is better from the New York native.

McBride has stretches where he's the team's best rate shooter and is a tremendously pesky point-of-attack defender… but is that not what the team is getting out of a taller two-guard in Shamet?

Clarkson has been a sparkplug every game he's played this year, but the 35-year-old showed his maddening inconsistency in his first year in New York.

It's created an extremely volatile rotation, which isn't surprising when you have a coach who opened the year saying only five players on this team have minutes that are secured.

Despite zero Knicks players averaging more than 33 minutes a game except Brunson (who's at 34.1), only Shamet is averaging more than 18 minutes a game off the bench.

McBride is at 17.5, Kolek is at 12.2, Alvarado is at 11.3, and Clarkson is at 9.9, but even these are deceiving.

Kolek has played steady rotation minutes, but didn't play at all in the first two games before Alvarado's ankle injury. Alvarado played 16 and 11 minutes, got hurt, got benched after nine minutes on Monday, and was on track to be a DNP-CD against the Bulls on Wednesday.

Clarkson's gone from playing three, five, or eight minutes to his spurts of 15 and 17 in the two games against the Bulls and his 11 to spark the incredible fourth quarter against Brooklyn.

McBride, who's served as the Knicks' sixth man for over two years now, has topped 17 minutes just twice. He played a season-low 11 minutes on Wednesday and hasn't had a clear role.

To get these guys more minutes, it would mean cutting minutes from Hart or Bridges. While both have their warts, and the latter continues to look somehow less involved than he was during his lowest moments last season, some of the Knicks' best lineups are coming with them on the court… just separated.

We've seen Brown deploy line changes significantly more in the early going, opting to sub in three or four players at a time to deploy uniform units, but that's not helping how erratic this all feels.

The depth is making players redundant on the court, especially with how unwise it is to run four-guard lineups in an NBA that's prioritizing size and length more and more.

And with the Knicks' extremely shallow forward and center depth? This glut might have to be sorted out at the trade deadline. As much as McBride has shown his tremendous value to the Knicks over the course of his contract, if he isn't going to play more, is it even a net positive to keep him for the playoff run?

For now, it's merely a conundrum that Coach Brown will be scratching his head over every game.

By the time we hit February? Things better be sorting themselves out.

#2931. Sen. Chris Murphy released from hospital after stroke, will miss votes for 'several weeks' as he rehabs

Connecticut Democratic Sen. Chris Murphy said Friday he had been released from a hospital after suffering a stroke last weekend.

Murphy, 53, said he will be in a rehabilitation center for the next "several weeks," meaning he will miss votes in the Senate when Congress returns on Monday.

"The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I've made so far," he said in a statement.

"In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today," said Murphy, who has been discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028.

Dr. Jose Suarez, chief of Neurosciences Critical Care at The Johns Hopkins Hospital, in a statement released by Murphy's office, said, "Senator Murphy experienced a small hemorrhagic stroke."

"He has shown tremendous progress, and at the current rate of improvement, we expect that he will make a full recovery following rehabilitation," Suarez said.

Murphy had been in the hospital since having a stroke at his son's hockey game.

"Last Saturday afternoon, I felt something was not right with my body and called 9-1-1 out of an abundance of caution, and I'm glad I did," Murphy said in his statement. "I was diagnosed with a small bleed in the thalamus region of my brain."

Murphy's stroke came three days before Democratic candidates, in a stunning election result, won more than enough races to flip the Senate and House of Representatives from Republican majority control in January.

Murphy, who was not up for reelection this year, before the election had campaigned for Democratic candidates around the country, most recently in Michigan, Minnesota and Florida. He had been scheduled to campaign in New Hampshire on Sunday with Rep. Chris Pappas, who on Tuesday won election to the Senate.

#2928. Giannis is playing defense like a toddler chasing a ball

It wasn't long ago that Giannis Antetokounmpo was one of the NBA's best defenders. He joined Michael Jordan and Hakeem Olajuwon as the third player ever to be named NBA MVP and Defensive Player of the Year in the same season during the 2019-2020 campaign, and he was consistently a major part of some great units in Milwaukee. Antetokounmpo was always at his best as a 'roamer' who could run around and make plays next to Brook Lopez's reliable rim protection in drop coverage.

Giannis is still roaming after his offseason trade to the Miami Heat, but he doesn't look like the same defender he once was now that he's on the brink of turning 32 years old. Antetokounmpo ranks outside of the top-100 in Dean Oliver's Net-Points Defense stat, and he put some ugly flashes on tape Thursday night as the Golden State Warriors beat the Heat, 120-100.

Antetokounmpo went viral on Thursday for an ugly clip of chasing the ball defensively with absolutely zero purpose. Giannis looked like a big golden retriever out there following the ball wherever it went without actually positioning himself to help make a defensive stop. Watch the clip here:

Giannis looks tired as hell in this clip, and that's understandable. He's the only real offensive creator on the Heat right now, and he's not working with the type of spacing he enjoyed during his heyday with the Bucks. He's putting up a 32.8% usage rate right now, and it's probably too much to ask to carry that level of offensive burden while also being a great defender.

Sharp observers have noticed Giannis' defense starting to decline the last couple seasons. The Heat's best pathway to success this season was being an elite defensive team, and I'm just not sure if they can get there. Miami fell to 3-6 after the loss to the Warriors, and they're lucky to even be that good, because they needed an overtime buzzer-beater to defeat a terrible Los Angeles Clippers team in their previous game.

There's a lot of basketball left to be played, but the Giannis trade feels like a lose-lose for Milwaukee and Miami so far.

#2927. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2926. Eagles Film Review: Joe Milton is an easy player to get excited about

This week got away from me. Life has been ridiculously busy, and I couldn't put together my usual weekly preview article. But with the Eagles turning to Joe Milton at quarterback, I still wanted to give you something. So, let's do this! Milton is an easy player to get excited about and a lot of fun to watch. I enjoyed this one.

The first thing you notice is the arm. Milton can rifle short and intermediate throws without needing a big wind-up or much of a step into the ball. It almost looks like a flick of the wrist, and the ball arrives with unusual zip. The release is quick, too. He does not need a long gathering motion to generate velocity, which matters when a defender is closing or when the offense wants the ball out before the underneath coverage can rally.

That quick release could also make the Eagles' RPO game more dangerous. A bubble or slant has to get out before the defender responsible for the receiver can drive downhill. Milton has the arm to deliver those throws quickly, and the ball can arrive before the defense has time to recover. The speed of the delivery gives the concept a chance to punish a defense for overcommitting to the run.

Receivers may need to adjust, because the ball comes in hot, but this is a rare physical tool. It jumps out on film. The same velocity that makes an RPO throw difficult to defend can also make an ordinary catch harder if the ball is high or behind the receiver.

The arm also lets Milton challenge tight windows. On this rep, he sees the linebacker dropping underneath and trusts that he can beat him to the spot. The ball gets there with enough speed to take away the defender's chance to close the space.

That is the frustrating part of his film. His accuracy is not uniformly bad. He can put the ball exactly where it needs to go, then miss the next one by a mile.

Milton can drive the ball downfield, too. The Eagles have been short on vertical passing lately, and this preseason throw shows why they should give him chances to attack deep. He drops the ball into the bucket with both velocity and placement. This isn't just about throwing it far; he has to put enough air and location on it for the receiver to run onto the ball while keeping the defender from making a play.

A strong arm can force a defense to respect the deep part of the field, but a deep passing game only works if the quarterback can pair range with precise ball location. Milton shows that combination here. The Eagles have not given us much vertical offense recently, and I need to see some of it. I'm hopeful Milton will throw some deep bombs this week!

Comfortable on the move and patient in the pocket

I expect the Eagles to use more play-action bootlegs with Milton. He looked comfortable throwing on the move throughout the preseason, and this was supposed to be a bigger part of the offense already. Boot action can make the defense honor the run, change the quarterback's launch point, and create a simpler picture outside the pocket. Milton can sell that action and still deliver the ball while moving, which gives the play a chance to stress the edge defender and the coverage behind him.

If the Eagles want to lean on play action, Milton's comfort on the move is a real reason to call it. Maybe we finally see more of it this week. If you can't run play-action bootlegs with Joe Milton, I'm not sure you will be able to do it with anyone…

Milton can run, but he does not seem eager to abandon the pocket at the first opportunity. Here, he eliminates the routes at the top of the screen, works back to the comeback, steps up to avoid the edge rusher, and keeps looking to throw. He could take off through the open lane, but he wants to win as a passer first. He is prepared to stay in the pocket, make space for the throw, and keep his eyes downfield.

He is particularly comfortable rolling to his left. This is a 25-yard completion on the move, and he makes it look easy. A right-handed quarterback throwing across his body in that direction can make the ball sail, but Milton stays balanced enough to put it on the receiver without stopping and resetting. He gets the throw away while still moving rather than needing to gather himself and give the defense extra time to close.

Throwing on the move while rolling to the left, for a right-handed quarterback, is one of the more difficult skills to execute. Milton can do it pretty easily. The Eagles can move him away from the original launch point without reducing the play to "run or throw it away." The film shows that he can keep the play alive as a passer.

Then there is the deep ball. On this 50-yard throw, Milton looks off the safety, takes the one-on-one shot, and puts the ball in the back corner of the end zone. He may be a beat late, but the arm strength gives him enough margin to complete a throw that would be unavailable to many quarterbacks. He has to get it over the coverage and still keep it in a spot where only his receiver can make the play.

The running ability is another obvious way to make this offense more interesting. Milton is a big, powerful quarterback who can move quickly in the open field. The 4.6 40 time is evident on film. The Eagles should design runs for him and use that threat to help the run game. If they cannot get a quarterback with his size and speed to create something on the ground, I am not sure what else they are waiting for.

I want to see some QB counter bash, QB draw, and good old-fashioned read-option runs. Those concepts make the defense account for Milton as a ball carrier instead of treating him as a passer who might scramble only when the play breaks down. On a read option, the edge defender has to respect the quarterback keep; on a designed draw or counter bash, Milton can become an extra runner in the blocking scheme. That can change the numbers in the box and create a conflict for the defense. It will not fix a struggling run game by itself, but it gives the Eagles another way to make the defense hesitate.

The biggest issue is inconsistent accuracy. Milton doesn't always miss by a little; when he does, the ball can be nowhere near the target. This is a wide-open deep crossing route with a clean pocket, and he sails it badly. There is no pressure forcing him to rush the throw in this example, and the receiver has room to work across the field. The ball simply does not give him a chance to complete it.

The arm strength makes the good throws spectacular, but you can't miss like this and play in this league. There is a reason a quarterback with this level of physical talent was available. He will miss some throws by a big distance.

The other obvious risk is that Milton trusts his arm enough to force throws that are not there. He can fit the ball into windows other quarterbacks cannot, but that confidence can turn into a turnover when the read or timing is wrong.

The Eagles have leaned toward a risk-averse style; I am not sure they can play exactly the same way with Milton. He is a boom-or-bust quarterback, and the coaching staff will have to accept some lows if it wants to access the highs. There is a balance to find: let him use the arm and create explosive plays, while giving him clear answers so he does not feel he has to force a hero throw on every difficult down.

I am not pretending Milton is a polished answer at quarterback. Obviously. The accuracy can be wildly inconsistent, and the bad plays are pretty rough. But there is legitimate talent here. He has a quick release, rare velocity, real downfield ability, and enough mobility to add a different dimension to the offense. The Eagles have spent too much of this season making the offense hard to watch. Milton gives them a chance to run more boot action, take vertical shots, and use the quarterback in the run game.

Will it be messy? Almost certainly. It could completely suck, let's be honest. This is a poorly schemed offense with a lot of talent issues right now. If they allow loads of unblocked pressure again, he probably won't have a chance to succeed. Milton's mobility may help him escape some of that pressure. But the protection and the play design still have to give him a chance to operate.

But if the Eagles cannot be good right now, I would at least like them to be fun. Why the hell not? Let Milton rip it. I've been so bored with the Eagles since Jalen Hurts has been unable to play. Joe Milton might just make it a little bit more fun, just not necessarily better.

Thank you for reading! I'd love to hear your thoughts, so feel free to comment below and ask any questions. If you would like to support me further, please check out my Patreon here!

#2924. OpenAI bugging suspect Alfozan pleads not guilty

The Saudi national charged with planting a listening device in the headquarters of OpenAI while he worked there pleaded not guilty on Friday during an appearance in San Francisco federal court.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, was sent back to jail after the brief hearing. Alfozan has been held without bail since his arrest on a criminal complaint in early October.

He faces a maximum possible sentence of five years in prison if convicted in the case.

A grand jury indicted Alfozan on Oct. 27 on a single count of interception of oral communications, court records show.

Prosecutors say that he put a recording device in a television in an executive conference room at OpenAI's offices in San Francisco on Sept. 28.

Alfozan's lawyer declined to comment after the hearing before Magistrate Judge Peter Kang.

The judge ordered Alfozan to next appear in court on Nov. 17.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm, Raed Ventures.

"He currently serves as Engineering Lead at OpenAI, with a focus on AI Agents and the data platform," Alfozan's bio page on Raed's site says.

Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of Saudi Arabia's royal family, is a venture partner in the same firm.

Alfozan's case comes as U.S. companies such as OpenAI are making rapid advancements in developing artificial intelligence agents, and as concerns grow about the potential theft of AI tech from those companies.In June, President Donald Trump, in an executive order on AI innovation, said it was U.S. policy "to protect American ingenuity and intellectual property from exploitation and theft by adversaries; and to cultivate America's advanced AI-enabled capabilities."Trump has had a close relationship with Saudi Arabia. But he has also taken steps during his second term in the White House to limit the number of visas for highly skilled foreign workers.OpenAI earlier this year held discussions with Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the country's sovereign wealth fund, about investing in the company.

— Jordan Novet reported from San Francisco

#2922. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

After three successive defeats in the Prem Rugby Cup, last season's winners Leicester are bottom of Pool B.

Leicester, well beaten, managed three tries, two from hooker Sam Moli and one from back row Osian Thomas.

Sam Worsley had a chance to win it for the Bears four minutes from time but was short with a long-range penalty attempt.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

#2918. How the 49ers plan to use DeForest Buckner with new look DL room

The San Francisco 49ers are hitting the field on Sunday against the Las Vegas Raiders, coming off a bye week, and they'll have their new addition on the defensive line with them. Recently traded defensive tackle DeForest Buckner is set to make his 49ers return, marking his first game in San Francisco uniform since the 2019 Super Bowl.

Buckner, 32, has long been known as a quality pass rusher with a unique size-length profile at 6'7, and will hope to add some of that to a young defensive tackle room that also includes Osa Odighizuwa. Now, the 49ers have their two veterans, along with rookie Gracen Halton and former undrafted free agent Sebastian Valdez as their top depth.

With the Indianapolis Colts, Buckner had a top five snap count among defensive tackles this season. With the 49ers, that'll hopefully drop as the 49ers rotate their linemen more. But, with Buckner and Odighizuwa both having familiarity playing as three-techniques, how will the 49ers use Buckner in his return?

"We've done so many different things, moved people around," defensive coordinator Raheem Morris said. "They have the ability to play three-technique. They have the ability to play nose. They have the ability to play four-eye, the ability to play on the edge. You've kind of seen these guys kind of move around all over the place, so I wouldn't really put them in a bucket.

"You're talking about a guy with supreme talent. Those guys kind of can do whatever you ask them to do. Traditionally he's been a three-technique around here, playing a huge role and doing some really good things. He'll do a lot of the same thing. I'm a big believer in doing what people do really well and we'll try to find out what he does really well again, implement them to what the guys have been doing and put those guys together and put them out there playing fast."

The 49ers have regularly been moving players around, kicking some of their bigger edges inside, while also creating different ways to rush the passer. The creativity should increase with Nick Bosa also trending to play, but it doesn't seem like Morris is concerned with the usage.

San Francisco has been without Bosa for a few weeks now, and their pass rush has struggled a bit as a result. Now, with him and Buckner both likely in the fold this week, what will that do for the defense?

"I don't know when it would be a bad time to get a guy like Buck," Morris added. "It's been a lot of fun to meet him the last couple of days and be around him and watch him practice and put him a part of it. Really feel like I'm further away from it than he is because of how long he spent here and the time that he spent here and how much he means to this organization and the people around him.

"So, it's been a lot of fun being around that guy and to have that whole unit being able to come back relatively healthy. To see these guys (Buckner and Bosa) probably for the first time altogether, I think altogether, for the first time this year, I think it'd be a lot of fun."

Now, the 49ers have two veterans in their defensive tackle room, which should only help in the development of younger guys like Mykel Williams, Halton, Valdez, Romello Height, and the rest of the room.

"Buck's done it at a very high level for such a long time," Morris continued. "I don't know if there's anybody in this building that can't pick something up from him. I just know how much, let's just go with the ultimate leader in Fred [Warner] and [Dre] Greenlaw, how much respect those guys have for him. So when you're talking about guys that have done a lot of really cool things at different positions that can learn from him, you can only imagine what the guys up front can get from him, just those guys being together.

"Having Osa being a vet and then having Buck come in with some different perspective, different realities of what he's been through and what he's done. To be able to come back into the fold and really pick it back up and fit right back in with the guy that's coached him, guys that he's familiar with, whether it be the head coach or his position coach, I think is always a really good thing."

The Niners will kick things off against the Raiders this weekend as they look to start the second half of the season hot. We'll get to see how Buckner looks returning to the 49ers, and whether the team still needs to make another move ahead of the deadline.

#2917. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2916. Guardians’ Emmanuel Clase Trial Gets Honest Answers From Bo Naylor

CLEVELAND, OHIO - JUNE 08: Bo Naylor #23 and Emmanuel Clase #48 of the Cleveland Guardians celebrate the team's 4-2 win over the Houston Astros at Progressive Field on June 08, 2025 in Cleveland, Ohio. (Photo by Nick Cammett/Getty Images)

Emmanuel Clase and Luis Ortiz are on trial in Brooklyn for an alleged pitch rigging scheme from their time pitching with the Cleveland Guardians.

On Thursday, former Guardians catcher Bo Naylor appeared as a defense witness (ahead of schedule so he could fly to Italy to get married this weekend).

Two other catchers are on the witness list — Austin Hedges and Mike Zunino — and will potentially give their testimony later in the trial.

But first it was Naylor who gave his witness to what he knew about what had occurred.

It's certainly a notable perspective on the case to hear from a catcher who was behind the plate when Clase allegedly committed the pitch rigging.

GettyCLEVELAND, OHIO – JUNE 08: Bo Naylor #23 and Emmanuel Clase #48 of the Cleveland Guardians celebrate the team's 4-2 win over the Houston Astros at Progressive Field on June 08, 2025 in Cleveland, Ohio. (Photo by Nick Cammett/Getty Images)

The Athletic's Sam Blum is covering this trial and provided great details on what was said during Thursday's testimony.

Here's a portion of what he wrote about Naylor:

After watching a video of the allegedly rigged pitch that he caught, Naylor testified it appeared he called the pitch — an outside slider to St. Louis Cardinals hitter Pedro Pagés — using PitchCom. Naylor had already testified that Pages' scouting report suggested low-and-away sliders were Pages' most prominent weakness as a hitter.

"Seems like a pretty good pitch to have as a weapon if you want to induce chase," Naylor testified in the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of New York in Brooklyn.

Blum goes on to write that the defense attorney for Clase pointed out that five of the seven at bats Naylor was catching in which an allegedly rigged pitch occurred still resulted in outs.

Blum writes: "That questioning appeared to be part of a larger strategy to show that throwing pitches off the plate, even intentionally, can still be in furtherance of trying to get the hitter out."

And then Blum added this about the prosecution's questioning of Naylor:

On cross-examination by the government, Naylor was shown video of Clase appearing to shake off signals on two allegedly rigged pitches, implying that Clase was making a conscious decision to throw pitches slower and for a ball, which would lead to payouts on the bets.

The prosecution asked Naylor about Clase's elite command of pitches, but Naylor said Clase was better able to command location on his cut fastball than on his slider.

Once Naylor was done, the trial went back to the prosecution's case, since Naylor had simply gone out of order ahead of his international flight.

The prosecution will have to continue its case before the defense gets to call its other witnesses.

There will eventually be a verdict, and no matter what happens, it's a hugely important case for the future of Major League Baseball and sports betting.

#2915. A disturbing employment trend is hiding beneath the latest strong job numbers

Americans who lose their jobs these days are facing a harsh new problem: It's getting harder to find another one quickly.

The average jobless worker's bout of unemployment surpassed 27 weeks in October on a seasonally adjusted basis, according to Friday's jobs report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That amounts to a job search of nearly seven months, the longest outside of the pandemic period since 2016, when the U.S. labor market was finally getting back on track after the 2008 financial crisis.

About 2 million Americans have been without work for at least 27 weeks, the BLS reported. People in this category are referred to as the "long-term unemployed" and accounted for 27.6% of all jobless workers in October, the highest share since late 2021.

"This is a series that can be noisy from month to month," said Cory Stahle, senior economist at Indeed, a hiring platform. "But if you look over a longer time horizon, there is no mistake in the trend."

It's a troubling sign in an otherwise steady labor market. Workers stuck on the sidelines are a casualty of what's become a "low-hire, low-fire" job market, in which companies are hesitant to lay workers off, but aren't adding them either.

Research shows that Americans who experience long-term unemployment face depleted savings, lower earnings potential over time and declining mental health.

What's more, workers who are unemployed for extended periods risk falling into a self-perpetuating cycle, researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond wrote earlier this year.

"Time spent unemployed may itself reduce the probability of finding a job, perhaps because skills depreciate, networks thin or employers interpret long unemployment durations as a negative signal," the researchers warned.

Workers stop being able to claim most, if not all, government unemployment benefits after 26 weeks in a year. Given that, a rising share of long-term unemployed people can explain in part why initial and continuing jobless claims have remained low.

"Now, the average unemployed person takes longer to find a job than their benefits would last," Stahle said. "That is a really key point of this."

While jobless workers appear to be having a tough time, this week's employment data shows strength in other areas of the labor market.

Nonfarm payrolls blew past economists' forecasts for October and added 165,000 jobs, according to Friday's release. The unemployment rate ticked down to 4.1%, slightly lower than economists predicted.

Job openings came in much higher than economists expected in September, according to a separate report from the BLS released on Tuesday. The number of workers quitting jobs — a barometer of their confidence to obtain new employment — spiked to levels not seen since March 2025.

The BLS also said layoffs and discharges dropped to their lowest volume in more than a year. However, a recent string of high-profile workforce cuts could throw cold water on views among workers that the market is heating up.

CNBC reported this week that Disney is planning to axe thousands of jobs, the latest of several waves of layoffs at the media giant this year. American Express said last month that it was slashing head count by 5% as it reorients around artificial intelligence.

"It's difficult to separate what's happening to you or the people around you from the big picture," said Elizabeth Renter, lead economist at Life360. "When your LinkedIn is filled with layoffs and your social circle features a few folks who have been job seeking for several months, positive narratives about the labor market can seem jarring."
