#2914. Packers Injury Report: 5 are out vs. the Patriots on Sunday

The Green Bay Packers have announced their initial game statuses for Sunday's action against the New England Patriots, following a week of practice. Five players have already been ruled out: tight end Josh Whyle (concussion), offensive lineman Travis Glover (knee), offensive lineman Sean Rhyan (ankle), linebacker Zaire Franklin (shin), and edge defender Micah Parsons (knee). Parsons remains on the physically unable to perform list, while the other four players are on the 53-man active roster.

With four scratches already, the Packers are down to 49 options for their 48 players on game-day already, and that's if their questionable players suit up: running back MarShawn Lloyd (knee) and cornerback Keisean Nixon (groin). Running back Chris Brooks, who is on the injured reserve at the moment but has his practice window open, is also listed as questionable.

Green Bay is not likely to carry more than three running backs or eight offensive linemen on the 48-man game-day roster, so I bet there's at least one call-up from the practice squad this week, most likely to help on special teams while Isaiah McDuffie and Ty'Ron Hopper are full-time starting linebackers. This has been what the team's plan has been in recent weeks.

On the Patriots' end of the injury report, receiver A.J. Brown (ankle/knee) and running back TreVeyon Henderson (illness) have been ruled out for Week 9. Players listed as questionable by New England are safety Kevin Byard III (chest), cornerback Carlton Davis III (neck), tight end Hunter Henry (foot), receiver Mack Hollins (calf), tackle Morgan Moses (foot), safety Brenden Schooler (hamstring) and offensive lineman Greg Van Roten (hamstring).

#2913. Doc Rivers talks Giannis Antetokounmpo leaving Bucks, challenges of coaching superstar

Giannis Antetokounmpo is one-of-one. He doesn't play in the style of other superstars, which makes coaching the NBA champion and two-time MVP a particular challenge.

Doc Rivers, the former Bucks coach turned NBC Sports studio host for NBA broadcasts, pulled no punches when talking about how he found it difficult to coach Antetokounmpo in an appearance on the Bill Simmons podcast.

"I liked Giannis a lot. I liked him more as a dude. He's interesting. He's not easy. He's not easy because he plays at this crazy downhill pace. He's really a difficult guy....

"He has this great passing ability, but he doesn't necessarily have this super IQ at the same time. It's really a strange thing, and he really has one way: downhill, force, force. Every play, he wants to force it. I wish he learned how to execute better. He's one of those guys... that have never needed to execute because they can do it on their own. But that doesn't help the team. And I told Giannis this, the thing he needs to still learn is the value of execution. How important it is when you're in practice and shoot-arounds to lock in on execution. And now he has a coach [Erik Spoelstra] who is as good as there is in execution."

Rivers came in at the end of Antetokounmpo's time in Milwaukee, taking over in the middle of the 2023-24 season, and the team never found a consistent identity under Rivers' leadership. Part of that was the personnel around Antetokounmpo at the time: Jrue Holiday was gone, Kris Middleton was battling injuries and not the same player as his peak, and Damian Lillard was supposed to be the "fix."

Rivers also was there for Antetokounmpo's exit from Milwaukee, and he talked about that as well with Simmons.

"Giannis wanted out, but he was a Buck. And he didn't want to be the bad guy. He wanted [GM Jon Horst and owner Wes Edens] and those guys to say, 'We're trading you,' instead of him asking for a trade. To me, that was the game that was being played."

That take is not controversial, that's how pretty much everyone saw Antetokounmpo's exit from Milwaukee.

His comments on Antetokounmpo's play are going to draw pushback out of Miami.

#2911. Minkah Fitzpatrick: No controversy with my quote, everyone on the Jets knows what I meant

After Sunday’s loss to the Raiders, Jets safety Minkah Fitzpatrick said something that caused a bit of a controversy, but Fitzpatrick says there was no controversy with the only people who matter to him.

Fitzpatrick said what sounded like the loss was a case of the “Jets being Jets,” but after that quote took off, the Jets told reporters that Fitzpatrick had actually said, “Jets beating Jets.” Some thought Fitzpatrick was saying the Jets are fundamentally a bad franchise and that losing like they did is just who they are, but the Jets insisted Fitzpatrick was saying the Jets just need to cut down on their mistakes and stop beating themselves.

Asked about the quote on Thursday, Fitzpatrick said it’s a non-issue with his teammates, and that’s what’s important to him.

“It hasn’t really caused any stir within this locker room, and I think that’s the most important thing. Everyone in this locker room knew what I was talking about,” Fitzpatrick said. “Everybody within this locker room knew exactly what I said and what I was trying to communicate, and in my opinion that’s all that matters.”

Fitzpatrick said his teammates aren’t upset and the focus is on winning on Sunday.

“I know what I said, and that’s all I’m going to say,” Fitzpatrick said. “It doesn’t matter. We’re playing Kansas City this week. The locker room knows what I said, or what I meant to say, however you want to put it. Either way, it doesn’t matter. This locker room needs to focus on winning right now, and not something that I might have not said or something I might have done. Our focus and our objective now is to win and figure out what we need to win, not focus on shoulda, woulda, coulda.”

Ultimately, Fitzpatrick said, what the 2-6 Jets need is to get to 3-6 on Sunday.

#2907. Raptors look to claim first NBA Cup win

The Toronto Raptors are looking to secure their first win in the Emirates NBA Cup this season as they take on the Milwaukee Bucks on Friday, November 6.

The Raptors, who are currently 0-1 in the competition and are coming off of a 128-109 loss against the Denver Nuggets. Toronto has not been on great form, losing 3 of their last four and currently sit at 4-4.

For the Bucks, their start to the season has been poor as they enter this contest with a record of 1-7 and are on a four-game losing streak.

The Raptors have had many issues to start their season off, mainly surrounding slow starts to games, while not being able to finish games. Perimeter defence has also been a problem.

There has also been massive vulnerability in the frontcourt and rebounding. While they're strong on the offensive glass, their undersized or banged-up frontline has struggled to contain dominant opposing centres. With no true centre, the Raptors have struggled. They should be looking to make a trade for a well renowned centre to help them fix their issue there.

Not having Kawhi Leonard has also seemed to affect the Raptors, as there is now more pressure on Scottie Barnes, Immanuel Quickley, and RJ Barrett to carry the load.

For the Bucks, life without Giannis Antetokounmpo has been difficult. They are fielding a younger squad and are in a transitional period.

The core now features players acquired in recent moves, such as Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., and Kel'el Ware from Miami, alongside veterans like Myles Turner and coach Doc Rivers (with assistant Darvin Ham) trying to install a new system.

General execution and youth consistency have also been issues early in the year, the young and reshaped rotation (including rookie Brayden Burries and Ryan Rollins) has struggled with late-game execution, shooting efficiency, and closing out tight contests.

BOSTON, MA – NOVEMBER 4: Tyler Herro #11 of the Milwaukee Bucks handles the ball during the game against the Boston Celtics on November 4, 2026 at TD Garden in Boston, Massachusetts. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Brian Babineau/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The key to this matchup lies in the battle for possession, where Toronto's elite offensive rebounding and low turnover rates are heavily favoured to exploit Milwaukee's 27th-ranked defence.

To stay competitive, the Bucks must rely on Myles Turner and Kel'el Ware to control the glass and prevent Scottie Barnes and RJ Barrett from dominating the paint, while forcing Toronto into a perimeter shooting contest where they currently hit just 31.9 percent from deep.

Ultimately, if Milwaukee's Tyler Herro and AJ Green can capitalize on high-efficiency looks from three-point range to offset Toronto's relentless downhill physicality, the Bucks can mask their defensive deficiencies and stall the Raptors' high-volume possession game.

This is a great time for the Bucks to win their second game of the year, as they face a Toronto side that has really struggled as of late.

For the Raptors, they poses as a great get back game as they try to get back on track this season and avoid falling under .500 for the first time.

#2906. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2898. Florida high school football’s new Open Division is loaded: Meet the 8-team field

The Florida High School Athletic Association is introducing a new postseason format in 2026, bringing eight of the state's top high school football programs together to compete for the inaugural Open Division championship.

The field features an intriguing mix of nationally ranked contenders, traditional state powers and programs looking to prove they belong among Florida's best. Spoiler: There are a ton of heavy hitters.

Here's a look at the eight teams that made the inaugural FHSAA Open Division playoff bracket:

As expected, St. Thomas Aquinas enters the inaugural Open Division as Florida's top-ranked program. The Raiders finished the regular season undefeated at 10-0 and holds the No. 2 spot in the latest USA TODAY Super 25 rankings and the top spot in the USA TODAY Southeast Super 15. Having already beaten Columbus and Chaminade-Madonna this season, Roger Harriott's group enters the gauntlet trying to finish 15-0 but will be without Notre Dame commit Julius Jones Jr. after season-ending surgery earlier this year. Expect the defense and the run game to try and win games for the Raiders.

The Explorers have been outstanding this season under Dave Dunn, finishing out with a 9-1 record with the lone loss coming to St. Thomas Aquinas in Week 3. Columbus currently sits at No. 5 in the USA TODAY Super 25, and second in the USA TODAY Super 15 Southeast region. The Explorers stunned then-No. 1 nationally ranked Bishop Gorman (NV) 33-28 in August, announcing themselves as a legitimate national contender. Expect 5-star ATH A'mir Sears and QB Kai Moore to make their presences felt in these games.

The Bulls are off back-to-back state titles and enter the postseason with a 9-1 record. West Boca went unbeaten against in-state competition, having only lost to St. John's (DC) by one point in a 15-14 loss. The defense of Dylan Potts' squad has been elite this year, allowing just 46 points over 10 games. Expect this unit to be tested against some high-octane offenses, but the offensive unit led by QB Trey Moran, WR Billy Clancy III, and others can score points too.

The Lions have put together one heck of a season built by transfers, going 8-2 with losses to top-seeded St. Thomas Aquinas and Sierra Canyon (CA) as part of the Broward Showcase. The Lions are 13th in the USA TODAY Super 15 Southeast rankings, fueled by QB Brady Quinn and a wide receiver corps of Tromon Isaac, Kedarius Rhaney, and Armani Strong along with RB Amos Bradford. The Lions defeated American Heritage 46-27 and closed their regular season with four consecutive victories.

Lakeland has put together an outstanding season, finishing 9-1 with a double-overtime loss to Venice in Week 2. The Dreadnaughts have leaned on the versatility of senior Kelin Hendrix, who has contributed as both a passer and runner, throwing 17 touchdown passes while adding 10 rushing touchdowns. Senior running back Xavion Mitchell has provided another dimension to the offense, leading the team with 638 rushing yards and nine rushing touchdowns. Lakeland closed its regular season with a 35-0 shutout of The First Academy, effectively eliminating the Lions from Open Division contention.

Miami Central has perhaps the most challenging résumé of any team entering the Open Division with three regular season losses but enter as the No. 6 seed in the Open Division. The Rockets opened their schedule with a 23-22 road victory over Orange Lutheran (CA) before traveling north to face Buford (GA), falling 18-7. They also suffered a 28-3 loss to fellow Open Division qualifier Columbus and closed the regular season with a 27-10 defeat against Carol City. Those results tell part of the story, but Central also demonstrated its offensive ceiling with a 52-10 victory over Northwestern and a 63-7 win against Key West.

Raines might be bringing the most momentum of any team in the inaugural field. The Vikings finished 10-0, extending their overall winning streak to 24 games and completing their third consecutive undefeated regular season. Their calling card has been a defense capable of changing games in a hurry, allowing just 10.7 points per game with 19 takeaways this year. Quarterback Samon Ellison-Morgan and running back TJ Williamson provide the offensive production, while defensive lineman Chenaniah Doran and edge rusher Troy Butler have been disruptive up front.

Venice has a legitimate offensive centerpiece in senior running back Dorien Irving-Jones, who has been one of the most productive backs in Southwest Florida this season. Irving-Jones averaged 148.6 rushing yards per game and accounted for 24 total touchdowns, giving the Indians a reliable offensive identity built around their ground attack. Senior Ira Dale has provided another rushing option, while freshman receiver and return specialist Mike Blakley has emerged as an intriguing playmaker with 63.3 receiving yards per game and six receiving touchdowns.

Just missed: Carrollwood Day (Tampa, FL), Cardinal Newman (West Palm Beach, FL), American Heritage (Plantation, FL)

Note: Any changes from the most recent injury report will be bolded.

"I think (Brian Branch) BB's going to be good here. We'll get through today, and I think we'll be OK."

#2893. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2891. Giannis Antetokounmpo is ‘difficult’ to coach: Doc Rivers

Retired NBA coach Doc Rivers is never afraid to speak the truth, and Giannis Antetokounmpo is on his mind these days.

Rivers explained on the "Bill Simmons Podcast" that Antetokounmpo is a "difficult guy" after having coached him for a season and a half before retiring in April 2026.

"He's not easy," Rivers said of coaching Antetokounmpo. "He plays at this crazy downhill pace. He's really a difficult guy. He has this great passing ability, but he doesn't have super IQ at the same time. He really has one way. Every play, he wants to force it."

Giannis Antetokounmpo of the Miami Heat shoots a free throw during a game against the Golden State Warriors in San Francisco on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Antetokounmpo is an ultra-rare physical specimen, earning the nickname "The Greek Freak," standing 6-foot-11 and weighing 250 pounds.

His time in Milwaukee is mostly considered a success, as he won an NBA title in 2021, but the end of his career with Rivers was frustrating. The Bucks had nearly no assets to acquire new players and Antetokounmpo himself was a constant source of trade conjecture.

"I wished he learned how to execute better," Rivers said. "The thing he needs to still learn is the value of execution. How important it is when you're in practice and shoot around to lock in on execution. Now he has a coach who's as good as there is in execution."

The Bucks finally did trade him to the Miami Heat this past offseason and he's hooked up with coach Erik Spoelstra, though early returns have not been good with a 3-6 record.

"For the team to work, you have to learn how to execute," Rivers said.

Rivers has always been honest when talking about players he's coaching, including when he's still with the team.

Head coach Doc Rivers and Giannis Antetokounmpo #34 of the Milwaukee Bucks look on against the Brooklyn Nets during the game at Barclays Center on December 08, 2024 in New York Getty Images

As the 76ers coach, Rivers had a blunt answer to a reporter after Ben Simmons passed up a wide-open layup in Game 7 of a playoff game against the Atlanta Hawks in 2021.

The reporter asked whether Rivers still believed in Simmons to be an elite point guard on a championship-winning team.

"I don't know that question or the answer to that right now," Rivers replied after they lost to a clearly inferior Hawks team that went on to not be a contender in the years following. "So I don't know the answer to that."

Simmons refused to show up the following year and was eventually traded to the Nets for James Harden before falling out of the league in a detrimental chain of events for his career.

Simmons later said Rivers' comments infuriated him.

Doc Rivers has never been one to clam up when talking about his players. USA TODAY Sports via Reuters Con

"How did I feel? I was like, 'What a piece of s—t," Simmons said of Rivers' blunt comments in a recent documentary "Ben Being Ben". "When times get tough, people start panicking. People throw people under the bus, and that's just what happened."

Antetokounmpo and the Heat face the floundering Kings on Sunday after losing to the Warriors on Thursday.

#2890. Jerry Jones scrambles to rectify his comments about coaching pay

Jerry Jones is trying to put the toothpaste back in the glory hole.

On Tuesday, Jones made a nonsensical case for the notion that there’s “zero correlation” between coaching pay and wins. On Friday, Jones argued that he’s willing to pay for coaches — notwithstanding his unmistakable claim that there’s no connection between paying big money to coaches and getting big results.

“No one will ever know if I’ve ever been interested in somebody that at the time was known to have, let’s say, a substantive financial marketplace,” Jones said in his latest appearance on 105.3 The Fan in Dallas. “No one will ever know. Well, the facts are I have been out there, and I have been trying to lure very successful coaches away from the teams they were on. And knew full well that I was going to pay them for all their success. . . . It is absolutely ridiculous, and I actually grin when somebody says, ‘Jerry’s not willing to pay the money to get the right kind of coach out there. . . .’ I have five wells that cost more than any coach I know of, each one of them, in the NFL.”

He can grin all he wants. But he said what he said. And now he’s saying something different because the reaction was universal.

There’s absolutely a connection between paying for a good coach and reaping the rewards of having one.

Pointing to his oil wells is gaslighting. Jones personally doesn’t see the benefit in paying a lot of money for an accomplished coach. So he’d rather treat the pursuit of a good coach like prospecting, searching for a low-rent coach who generates penthouse results.

The other factor is the power that flows from signing a coach to a market-level deal. Enough power to ignore Jones and to coach the team as he sees fit, confident that Jerry won’t fire him and risk owing the full buyout.

So why did Jerry react the way he did? He needs the fans to think he’s “all in” when it comes to chasing a championship (or, at a minimum, the franchise’s first berth in the NFC Championship in 31 years and counting). The moment fans realize that it’s all bluster and carnival barking and penny pinching is the moment they’ll stop going to Cowboys games and start looking for another team to support.

Tuesday’s comments were far too revealing. And, for loyal fans who give their time, money, and emotion to the Cowboys, far too damning regarding the manner in which the General Manager who can’t be fired is mismanaging the team.

#2888. Antonio Brown pleads guilty to aggravated assault with a firearm

Former NFL receiver Antonio Brown, who was charged with attempted murder after a May 2025 shooting in Miami, has pleaded guilty to a lesser charge.

Via ESPN, Brown pleaded guilty to aggravated assault with a firearm on Friday. He was sentenced to three years probation.

Conditions include attending weekly counseling sessions and not possessing a firearm.

If all terms are met, the probation could end in roughly 18 months. Also, the guilty plea to a felony charge would not be on his official record.

“Antonio and I are extremely grateful that this matter has finally been resolved. He faced a thirty-year prison sentence, including a twenty-year mandatory minimum,” Brown’s attorney, Mark Eiglarsh, said in a statement issued to ESPN. “The prosecutors offered to reduce the charge and allow him to serve probation without a conviction. Given the stakes, it was an offer we couldn’t refuse.”

The case would have hinged on the application of Florida’s “stand your ground” law, given that Brown claimed he fired a weapon at another man in self-defense. The facts would have made it difficult for Brown to show that he believed he was facing death or serious injury; he allegedly grabbed a gun from a security guard and fired at a man after an alleged assault on Brown had ended and the target of the bullets had disengaged from Brown.

Brown spent 12 years in the NFL, playing for the Steelers, Raiders (although he never appeared in a regular-season game), the Patriots, and the Buccaneers.

#2887. Report: Warriors expected to trade Kristaps Porzingis by December 16th

The Golden State Warriors Kristaps Porzingis saga is expected to come to an end before the end of the year, according to a report by Brett Siegel of ClutchPoints. Porzingis, who re-signed with the Dubs last season on a partially guaranteed two-year, $40 million deal, opted not to join the team for training camp and has accepted not receiving payments, seemingly deciding to retire. However, hoping to make the best out of an unexpected situation, the Warriors are hoping to use Porzingis' salary essentially as a trade exception. While that was all known, Siegel's reporting suggests the front office is expected to trade Porzingis by December 16th.

"For starters, Porzingis' time with the Warriors is over," Siegel wrote. "He will not be playing another game for this franchise, as ClutchPoints reported on Oct. 23 before the team's first game of the season, and it's almost a given he will be traded, likely within the next 30-40 days… Much like how the Warriors traded for Dennis Schroder in the middle of December during the 2024-25 season, only to re-aggregate him in a bigger deal for Jimmy Butler at the trade deadline, league sources are anticipating a similar strategy this time around with Porzingis."

Siegel notes that players who signed free agent contracts this offseason will all become trade-eligible on December 15th, which will also give teams more options on that day. Moreover, December 16th is the deadline for a player to be traded and still be eligible to be aggregated in a deal at the trade deadline in February. So while it's definitely possible for a deal to be made sooner, that effectively becomes the deadline for the Warriors front office if they would like to make a smaller move with Porzingis that would leave them flexibility to still go looking for bigger fish at the deadline.

Siegel's reporting does not even engage with a scenario where the Warriors do not trade Porzingis in December. It's worth wondering if they would simply hold onto him until the trade deadline if they did not receive an offer that enticed them enough before. With the Warriors 6-3 and looking like a legit playoff team, though, the front office should be less inclined to slow play adding depth to the roster.

#2886. NFL analyst praises Cam Heyward for continued dominance at 37 years old

Pittsburgh Steelers defensive tackle Cameron Heyward is one of the oldest active players in the NFL, but you wouldn't guess it from how incredible he's playing in 2026. That isn't lost on well-respected NFL analyst Brian Baldinger, who pointed out how Cameron Heyward continues to defy Father Time.

On a new episode of The Best Football Show podcast, Baldinger explained how blown away he's been with how elite Heyward is playing at 37 years old.

"Cam Heyward, he's in his 16th year, he might never have played better than he is right now," Baldinger said. "He's batting down balls. He's destroying guards. He ran right through the rookie guard [Austin] Barber, the left guard Zion Johnson. He destroyed those guys."

Heyward has been nothing short of dominant through eight games this season, recording 28 combined tackles, three tackles for loss, six batted-down passes, and 2.5 sacks.

Heyward is a major component of Pittsburgh's defensive resurgence in 2026, and it wouldn't be surprising if the veteran defensive tackle is able to add another first-team All-Pro honor to his seemingly Hall of Fame-worthy resume.

#2884. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2883. Sean McVay confirms Myles Garrett will play on Sunday

The Rams will be welcoming linebacker Myles Garrett back to their lineup on Sunday.

Head coach Sean McVay confirmed at a Friday press conference that Garrett will be activated from injured reserve ahead of this Sunday’s game against the Commanders. A report earlier in the day indicated that would be the case and the Commanders can now be certain that they have to plan for Garrett this weekend.

Garrett had knee surgery after Week 1 and returned to the practice field this week. The Rams will be hoping this run unfolds without further interruption and that he’ll be back to the level that earned him defensive player of the year honors with Cleveland in 2025.

In addition to being Garrett’s return, Sunday will also mark the first game that the Rams will have Garrett and Aaron Donald active at the same time.

McVay said that tight end Terrance Ferguson is set to come off of injured reserve as well. He’s missed five games with an ankle injury.

#2882. Taylor Swift called the 'fakest' celebrity fan by Knicks player

During a TikTok stream where the pair were playing NBA 2K, New York Knicks guards Tyler Kolek and Miles "Deuce" McBride were asked a question: who was the 'fakest' celebrity fan they encountered during the Knicks' championship run? Kolek didn't hesitate to name a major celebrity.

Kolek promptly named pop superstar Taylor Swift, who made headlines for her courtside appearance at Game 4 of the Knicks' series against the San Antonio Spurs on June 10, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Swift was spotted in a custom blue-and-orange T-shirt with the pun "Stevie Knicks," accompanied by her longtime friends Mariska Hargitay, Alana Haim, and Este Haim.

"I don't know if I've seen her at any other games… It's a little performative," Kolek said during the live stream.

McBride, laughing, agreed with Kolek's assessment but was quick to warn his teammate: "I would agree. The Swifties aren't going to like that answer."

Saving himself from the potential backlash, Kolek quickly clarified his remarks. He suggested that her busy schedule might simply prevent her from attending more often.

McBride teased Kolek for trying to backtrack and from the wrath of Swift's loyal fanbase.

#2881. Chicago Bears stadium proposal on Southeast Side gains attention as lawmakers work on legislation

A proposal for a new Chicago Bears stadium on the city's Southeast Side is gaining attention as lawmakers work on stadium legislation ahead of the fall veto session.

NFL Commissioner Roger Goodell accompanied Bears officials Kevin Warren and George McCaskey to a meeting with Illinois legislative leaders in Chicago earlier this week.

The discussions come as a new potential stadium location is being promoted at the former U.S. Steel site on the South Side.

Developer Related Midwest has released renderings of a proposed domed Bears stadium at the site. Company President Curt Bailey said the proposal has been presented to both the Bears and lawmakers who will return for the fall veto session later this month.

"It's iconic for the Bears to be in Chicago, to be on the lakefront. You think about those shots of the skyline and the lake and the Chicago Bears," Bailey said.

The stadium concept includes a window at the north end designed to showcase the Chicago skyline.

SEE ALSO | Chicago architecture firm proposes renderings of new Bears stadium near current White Sox location

Bailey said the Southeast Side location has been on the Bears' radar for four to five months. He said the property had previously been slated for a large data center project, but Related Midwest shifted plans as public sentiment toward such developments changed.

"When you talk about the the size of the the site, the ability to do all of the things around the stadium that you want to do, whether it's retail or entertainment, you know this site that we're talking about is 9 miles from Soldier Field," Bailey said.

Bailey noted the site is much closer to Soldier Field than the property the Bears already own in Arlington Heights.

READ MORE | Illinois lawmakers weigh stadium authority plan for Bears Arlington Heights option

In a statement, the Bears said they are continuing to pursue locations in Hammond, Indiana.

"The Chicago Bears remain committed to building a world-class stadium and mixed-use district for our fans, the Chicagoland community and the team. We continue to work collaboratively with local and state leaders in Indiana to advance our stadium development plans in Hammond, where we are finalizing due diligence details across two sites in the Wolf Lake area. At the same time, we appreciate the recent engagement from Illinois leadership to explore legislation which has allowed us to share our feedback. We expect productive dialogue with both leadership teams in the coming weeks. We have a responsibility and obligation to explore every option available to the club, and we look forward to developing a transformational project that will attract global events and deliver jobs and economic growth to all of Chicagoland," the statement said.

Bears officials have promised to continue conversations about a path forward for a stadium in Arlington Heights or Chicago, as well, according to ESPN's Ian Rapoport.

Gov. JB Pritzker said stadium legislation being developed by lawmakers could allow the Bears to pursue the Southeast Side location.

"This is up to the Bears. You know, there's a bill that's being put together that gives the Bears the opportunity to choose where they want to build a stadium in Illinois, and then it's going to be up to them where it goes," Pritzker said.

Pritzker said he wants the legislation to be ready during the first week of the veto session, which begins Nov. 17, so it could be voted on during the second week, which begins Dec. 1.
